marginal return in the population.  Had the heterogeneity been in $\eta$ (ability) rather than in
$r$, $S_j$ would be correlated with the error and OLS would be inconsistent.  The source of the
variation, not its size, is what determines whether the regression means anything.

\emph{(ii) But what it estimates is a preference parameter, not a technological one.}  The
marginal return is $r_j+\nu-g_w$, the individual's own discount rate adjusted for mortality and
wage growth.  A cross section in which schooling varies only because patience varies traces out
the \emph{marginal return schedule} $\Lambda'$, so the returns to schooling coefficient reveals
how impatient people are, not how productive schooling is.  The empirical range
$\gamma_s\in[0.06,0.10]$ then says that effective discount rates lie in roughly that range, which
is exactly the reading the text gives when it calibrates $r\approx0.10$, $\nu\approx0.02$ and
$g_w\approx0.02$ to obtain $\gamma_s\approx0.10$.

\emph{(iii) A single number conceals very heterogeneous returns.}  Take
$\eta(S)=\exp\bigl(aS-\tfrac{b}{2}S^{2}\bigr)$, so that $\Lambda'(S)=a-bS$ and
$S^{*}(r)=\bigl[a-(r+\nu-g_w)\bigr]/b$ is linear in $r$.  With $a=0.20$, $b=0.012$,
$\nu=g_w=0.02$ and $r$ spread uniformly over $[0.04,0.16]$, the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
r & 0.04 & 0.07 & 0.10 & 0.13 & 0.16\\
S^{*}\ \text{(years)} & 13.33 & 10.83 & 8.33 & 5.83 & 3.33\\
\text{marginal return} & 0.040 & 0.070 & 0.100 & 0.130 & 0.160
\end{array}
\]
and an OLS Mincer coefficient of $\wh\gamma_s=0.100$ --- the population mean.  But the marginal
return ranges from $4\%$ for the most-schooled to $16\%$ for the least-schooled.  A policy that
induces the impatient to stay one more year earns $16\%$; one that extends the schooling of the
already highly educated earns $4\%$.  Neither is $\wh\gamma_s$.  Figure~\ref{fig:ch10} shows the
gap: the true relation $\Lambda(S)$ is concave, and the fitted line crosses it twice.

\rmk{This is the theoretical core of the returns-to-schooling literature that follows Card
(1999): when marginal returns are heterogeneous, an instrument that shifts the \emph{cost} of
schooling recovers a local average of the returns for the individuals it moves, not a common
parameter --- and it can exceed the OLS estimate precisely because the individuals whose schooling
responds to cost shifters are the ones with the highest marginal returns.  The model here is the
simplest environment in which that statement is exactly true.}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch10_human_capital.pdf}
\caption{Left (Exercise 10.4): when schooling varies only because discount rates vary, the true
relation between log wages and schooling is the concave function $\log\eta(S)$, and the Mincer
coefficient is the average of the individual marginal returns.  Right (Exercise 10.19): in the
closed economy of Section 10.6, a rise in one group's ability shifts the accumulation map upward,
and the economy climbs to a higher $k^{*}$ --- raising the \emph{other} group's human capital from
the next period on.}
\label{fig:ch10}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.5}{}
The problem is
\[
\max_{[s(t)]}\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(r+\nu)t}\bigl(1-s(t)\bigr)h(t)\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
\dt h(t)=\phi\bigl(s(t)h(t)\bigr)-\delta_hh(t),\qquad s(t)\in[0,1],\ h(0)>0 .
\]

\emph{Step 1: the state is confined to a compact interval.}  Setting $s=1$ gives the fastest
possible accumulation, $\dt h\le\phi(h)-\delta_hh$.  Since $\phi$ is strictly concave, increasing
and eventually flat, $\phi(h)-\delta_hh$ is negative for large $h$; let $\bar h$ solve
$\phi(\bar h)=\delta_h\bar h$.  Then
\[
h(t)\le h_{\max}\equiv\max\{h(0),\bar h\}\qquad\text{for all }t .
\]
For a lower bound, note that the optimal path converges monotonically to the steady state
$h^{*}=\phi(x^{*})/\delta_h>0$ of \bk{(10.17)}--\bk{(10.18)}, so
$\inf_{t}h(t)=\min\{h(0),h^{*}\}>0$; pick $\epsilon$ strictly below that number.  This is the
argument of Exercise 7.27(c) applied here, and it is what the hint means by imitating Section 7.7.
The state therefore lives in the compact interval $[\epsilon,h_{\max}]\subset(0,\infty)$, and the
objective is bounded:
\[
\Bigl|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(r+\nu)t}(1-s)h\dd t\Bigr|\le\frac{h_{\max}}{r+\nu}<\infty .
\]

\emph{Step 2: \bk{Theorem 7.13} gives the necessary conditions.}  The control set $[0,1]$ is
compact, $G(h,s)=\phi(sh)-\delta_hh$ is continuously differentiable, the state is confined to a
compact interval, and the objective converges.  Theorem 7.13 therefore applies to the
current-value Hamiltonian
\[
\mathcal H(h,s,\mu)=(1-s)h+\mu\bigl[\phi(sh)-\delta_hh\bigr]
\]
and yields \bk{(10.14)}: $\mathcal H_s=0$, $\mathcal H_h=(r+\nu)\mu-\dt\mu$ and
$\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-(r+\nu)t}\mu(t)h(t)=0$.  The first is an equality rather than an inequality
because the optimum is interior: $\mathcal H_s=-h+\mu h\phi'(sh)$ is $+\infty$ at $s=0$ (since
$\phi'(0)=\infty$) and negative at $s=1$ (since $\phi'$ has fallen to zero by then), so no corner
can be optimal.

\emph{Step 3: \bk{Theorem 7.14} gives sufficiency --- after a change of variable.}  Theorem 7.14
requires the maximized Hamiltonian to be concave in the state.  As written, $\mathcal H$ is
\emph{not} jointly concave in $(h,s)$: the term $(1-s)h$ is bilinear, with Hessian
$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ and eigenvalues $\pm1$, and
$\phi(sh)$ is not jointly concave either.  Take instead the control
\[
x(t)\equiv s(t)h(t)\in\bigl[0,h(t)\bigr] ,
\]
which is the transformation the text introduces immediately after \bk{(10.14)}.  The problem
becomes
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(r+\nu)t}\bigl[h(t)-x(t)\bigr]\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
\dt h=\phi(x)-\delta_hh,\qquad 0\le x(t)\le h(t) ,
\]
whose Hamiltonian
\[
\mathcal H(h,x,\mu)=h-x+\mu\bigl[\phi(x)-\delta_hh\bigr]
\]
is \emph{linear} in $h$ and, since $\mu=1/\phi'(x)>0$ by \bk{(10.15)}, strictly concave in $x$:
jointly concave in $(h,x)$.  The constraint set $\{(h,x):0\le x\le h\}$ is convex, and the change
of variable is a bijection for $h>0$, so the two problems have the same solutions.

Finally, Theorem 7.14 needs $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-(r+\nu)t}\mu(t)h(t)\ge0$ along every feasible
path, which is immediate here because $h\ge0$ and $\mu>0$.  Hence the conditions \bk{(10.14)} are
sufficient as well as necessary, and strict concavity of $\phi$ makes the maximizing $x$ --- and
therefore the whole optimal path --- unique.

\rmk{The change of variable is usually presented as algebraic convenience, a way of reducing
\bk{(10.14)} to the two equations \bk{(10.15)} and \bk{(10.16)}.  This exercise shows that it is
doing something more substantial: it is what convexifies the problem.  Whenever a control enters
multiplicatively with the state, it is worth asking whether the product is the natural control.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.8}{}
Write $z=(k,h,i_k,i_h)$ and
\[
\wh H(z;\mu_k,\mu_h)=\underbrace{u\bigl(f(k,h)-i_h-i_k\bigr)}_{\textstyle =\,(u\circ g)(z)}
+\underbrace{\mu_h\bigl(i_h-\delta_hh\bigr)+\mu_k\bigl(i_k-\delta_kk\bigr)}_{\textstyle \text{linear in }z} ,
\qquad
g(z)\equiv f(k,h)-i_k-i_h .
\]
The second group of terms is linear in $z$ for any values of the costates, hence concave, so it
suffices to show that $u\circ g$ is concave.

\emph{$g$ is concave.}  $f$ is jointly strictly concave in $(k,h)$ by Assumptions 1$'$ and 2$'$,
and $-i_k-i_h$ is linear; a concave function plus a linear one is concave.

\emph{A concave increasing function of a concave function is concave.}  Let
$z_0,z_1$ and $\lambda\in[0,1]$, and write $z_\lambda=\lambda z_1+(1-\lambda)z_0$.  Then
\[
g(z_\lambda)\ \ge\ \lambda g(z_1)+(1-\lambda)g(z_0)
\]
by concavity of $g$; applying $u$, which is strictly increasing,
\[
u\bigl(g(z_\lambda)\bigr)\ \ge\ u\bigl(\lambda g(z_1)+(1-\lambda)g(z_0)\bigr)
\ \ge\ \lambda u\bigl(g(z_1)\bigr)+(1-\lambda)u\bigl(g(z_0)\bigr),
\]
the second inequality by concavity of $u$.  Hence $u\circ g$ is concave, and so is
$\wh H$. $\blacksquare$

Both hypotheses are needed: if $u$ were decreasing the first inequality would reverse, and if $u$
were convex the second would.  Assumption 3 supplies exactly $u'>0$ and $u''<0$.

Concavity of $\wh H$ in the states \emph{and} the controls jointly implies concavity of the
maximized Hamiltonian in the states (Exercise 7.7), which is what \bk{Theorem 7.14} requires; with
the transversality conditions, the conditions listed after \bk{(10.24)} therefore characterize the
optimum.

\rmk{$\wh H$ is concave but not \emph{strictly} so, and the failure is informative.  Along any
direction that changes $i_k$ and $i_h$ in opposite directions by the same amount, $g$ is constant
and $u\circ g$ is unchanged; only the linear terms move.  The composition of investment is
therefore not pinned down by concavity, which is precisely why the first-order conditions deliver
$\mu_k=\mu_h$ --- the two investments are perfect substitutes in the resource constraint --- and
why, as the text observes after \bk{Proposition 10.1}, the economy can jump instantaneously to the
balanced ratio with $i_k(0)$ very negative and $i_h(0)$ very large.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.9}{}
Write \bk{(10.25)} as $\Delta(k,h)=0$ with
\[
\Delta(k,h)\equiv f_k(k,h)-f_h(k,h)-(\delta_k-\delta_h) .
\]

\emph{Uniqueness.}  For fixed $k>0$,
\[
\frac{\partial\Delta}{\partial h}=f_{kh}(k,h)-f_{hh}(k,h)>0 ,
\]
since $f_{kh}>0$ by the maintained complementarity assumption and $f_{hh}<0$ by strict concavity.
So $\Delta(k,\cdot)$ is strictly increasing and has at most one zero.

\emph{Existence.}  By Assumption 2$'$, $f_h(k,h)\to\infty$ as $h\to0$ while $f_k(k,h)$ stays
finite, so $\Delta(k,h)\to-\infty$; and as $h\to\infty$, $f_h(k,h)\to0$ while $f_k(k,h)$ increases
without bound (since $f_{kh}>0$; for $f=k^{\alpha}h^{\beta}$, $f_k=\alpha k^{\alpha-1}h^{\beta}\to\infty$),
so $\Delta(k,h)\to+\infty$.  By continuity there is exactly one root, which defines
$\xi(k)$.

\emph{Monotonicity and smoothness.}  $f$ is twice continuously differentiable and
$\Delta_h\neq0$, so the implicit function theorem (\bk{Theorem A.25}) makes $\xi$ continuously
differentiable, with
\[
\boxed{\ \xi'(k)=-\frac{\Delta_k}{\Delta_h}
=\frac{f_{kh}(k,h)-f_{kk}(k,h)}{f_{kh}(k,h)-f_{hh}(k,h)}\Bigg|_{h=\xi(k)}\ >\ 0\ }
\]
because $f_{kk}<0$, $f_{hh}<0$ and $f_{kh}>0$ make both numerator and denominator strictly
positive.

\emph{An illustration.}  With $f(k,h)=k^{\alpha}h^{\beta}$, $\alpha=\beta=1/3$, $\delta_k=0.08$
and $\delta_h=0.04$, the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
k & 0.5 & 1.0 & 2.0 & 5.0\\
\xi(k) & 0.5508 & 1.1302 & 2.3353 & 6.1819\\
\xi'(k) & 1.1377 & 1.1772 & 1.2294 & 1.3267
\end{array}
\]
matching a numerical derivative to six digits.  In the special case $\delta_k=\delta_h$ the locus
is exactly linear, $h=(\beta/\alpha)k$; a higher depreciation rate on physical capital tilts the
economy toward human capital, and here $h^{*}/k^{*}=1.667$ rather than $1$.

\emph{What the result says.}  Physical and human capital are always in balance, at a ratio
determined entirely by technology and depreciation, because the two are produced from the same
good at the same price and can be converted into each other freely.  The economy therefore has
only one effective state variable --- which is the content of \bk{Proposition 10.1} and the reason
imbalances require irreversibility (Exercise 10.14) or labor market frictions (Section 10.6).
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.10}{}
\emph{(a) Consumption.}  The first two conditions after \bk{(10.24)} give
$\mu_k(t)=\mu_h(t)=u'(c(t))\equiv\mu(t)$.  The third then reads
\[
f_k\bigl(k,h\bigr)u'(c)-\mu\delta_k=\rho\mu-\dt\mu
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=\rho+\delta_k-f_k(k,h).
\]
Since $\mu=u'(c)$,
\[
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=\frac{u''(c)\dt c}{u'(c)}=-\varepsilon_u(c)\frac{\dt c}{c},
\qquad
\varepsilon_u(c)=-\frac{u''(c)c}{u'(c)},
\]
so that, substituting $h=\xi(k)$ from Exercise 10.9,
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c(t))}
\Bigl[f_k\bigl(k(t),\xi(k(t))\bigr)-\delta_k-\rho\Bigr] .
\]

\emph{Capital.}  Along the optimal path $h(t)=\xi(k(t))$, so $\dt h=\xi'(k)\dt k$.  From
\bk{(10.21)} and \bk{(10.22)},
\[
i_k=\dt k+\delta_kk,
\qquad
i_h=\dt h+\delta_hh=\xi'(k)\dt k+\delta_h\xi(k),
\]
and substituting into the resource constraint \bk{(10.23)}, which binds,
\[
c+\dt k+\delta_kk+\xi'(k)\dt k+\delta_h\xi(k)=f\bigl(k,\xi(k)\bigr) .
\]
Collecting the terms in $\dt k$,
\[
\dt k(t)=\frac{1}{1+\xi'(k(t))}
\Bigl[f\bigl(k(t),\xi(k(t))\bigr)-\delta_h\xi(k(t))-\delta_kk(t)-c(t)\Bigr] ,
\]
which is the second equation of \bk{Proposition 10.1}.  The factor $1/[1+\xi'(k)]$ is the price of
raising $k$ by one unit: doing so requires $\xi'(k)$ additional units of human capital to keep the
economy on the locus.

\emph{Transversality.}  Integrating $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho+\delta_k-f_k$,
\[
e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)=\mu(0)\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{t}\bigl[f_k\bigl(k(s),\xi(k(s))\bigr)-\delta_k\bigr]\dd s\Bigr),
\]
so $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu_k(t)k(t)=0$ is equivalent to
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}k(t)\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{t}\bigl[f_k\bigl(k(s),\xi(k(s))\bigr)-\delta_k\bigr]\dd s\Bigr)=0 ,
\]
and the second transversality condition, in $h$, is then implied by $h=\xi(k)$ and
$\mu_h=\mu_k$.  The system is a planar one in $(k,c)$ with exactly the structure of the
one-sector neoclassical model of Chapter 8, so it has a unique steady state at
$f_k(k^{*},\xi(k^{*}))=\delta_k+\rho$, saddle-path stable, with monotone convergence. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The transversality condition as printed in \bk{Proposition 10.1} omits $\delta_k$ from the
exponent.  The derivation above shows that the correct exponent carries the depreciation rate,
$-\int_0^t[f_k-\delta_k]\dd s$, that is $-\int_0^tr(s)\dd s$ with $r=f_k-\delta_k$ the net
interest rate, exactly as in \bk{(8.32)} and \bk{Proposition 8.7}.  Since
$f_k-\delta_k\to\rho>0$ along the equilibrium path, both versions are satisfied by the optimal
path and nothing substantive turns on it.}

\emph{(b) The other form.}  Repeat the argument with the \emph{fourth} condition after
\bk{(10.24)} in place of the third:
\[
f_h(k,h)u'(c)-\mu\delta_h=\rho\mu-\dt\mu
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=\rho+\delta_h-f_h(k,h),
\]
so that
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c(t))}
\Bigl[f_h\bigl(k(t),\xi(k(t))\bigr)-\delta_h-\rho\Bigr].
\]
The two expressions are identical because subtracting the two costate equations gives
\[
f_k(k,h)-\delta_k=f_h(k,h)-\delta_h ,
\]
which is exactly \bk{(10.25)}.  The program confirms it: with $f=k^{1/3}h^{1/3}$,
$\delta_k=0.08$, $\delta_h=0.04$, the columns $f_k-\delta_k$ and $f_h-\delta_h$ agree to six
digits at every $k$ on the locus, and the steady state has
\[
k^{*}=61.728,\qquad h^{*}=102.881,\qquad
f_k(k^{*},h^{*})-\delta_k=f_h(k^{*},h^{*})-\delta_h=\rho=0.02 .
\]

\emph{Why.}  Both capitals are assets, both are produced from the consumption good one for one,
and both can be accumulated or decumulated freely.  No-arbitrage therefore forces their net rates
of return to be equal, and that common value \emph{is} the interest rate $r(t)$ the household
faces.  The Euler equation compares $r$ with $\rho$; it is indifferent about which of the two
assets one uses to measure $r$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.11}{}
Modify the resource constraint to $c+(1+\tau)(i_k+i_h)\le f(k,h)$, as in Section 8.10, and repeat
the optimization.  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H=u\bigl(f(k,h)-(1+\tau)(i_k+i_h)\bigr)+\mu_k(i_k-\delta_kk)+\mu_h(i_h-\delta_hh),
\]
and the first-order conditions in the two investments give
\[
\mu_k=\mu_h=(1+\tau)u'(c).
\]
The costate equation in $k$ is $f_ku'(c)-\mu_k\delta_k=\rho\mu_k-\dt\mu_k$, so
\[
\frac{\dt\mu_k}{\mu_k}=\rho+\delta_k-\frac{f_k(k,h)}{1+\tau} ,
\]
the distortion entering because a unit of forgone consumption now buys only $1/(1+\tau)$ units of
capital.  In a steady state $\dt c=0$, hence $\dt\mu_k=0$, and likewise for $h$:
\[
f_k(k^{*},h^{*})=(1+\tau)(\rho+\delta_k),
\qquad
f_h(k^{*},h^{*})=(1+\tau)(\rho+\delta_h).
\tag{$*$}
\]

\emph{Cobb--Douglas.}  With $Y=K^{\alpha}H^{\beta}L^{1-\alpha-\beta}$ and $L=1$, so
$y=k^{\alpha}h^{\beta}$, the two conditions in $(*)$ read $\alpha y/k=A_k$ and $\beta y/h=A_h$,
where $A_k\equiv(1+\tau)(\rho+\delta_k)$ and $A_h\equiv(1+\tau)(\rho+\delta_h)$.  Hence
$k=\alpha y/A_k$ and $h=\beta y/A_h$, and substituting back into $y=k^{\alpha}h^{\beta}$,
\[
y=\Bigl(\frac{\alpha y}{A_k}\Bigr)^{\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{\beta y}{A_h}\Bigr)^{\beta}
=y^{\alpha+\beta}\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{A_k}\Bigr)^{\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{A_h}\Bigr)^{\beta}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
y=\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{A_k}\Bigr)^{\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{A_h}\Bigr)^{\beta}
\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha-\beta}} .
\]
Both $A_k$ and $A_h$ are proportional to $1+\tau$, so $y\propto(1+\tau)^{-(\alpha+\beta)/(1-\alpha-\beta)}$
and
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{Y(\tau)}{Y(\tau')}=\Bigl(\frac{1+\tau'}{1+\tau}\Bigr)^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{1-\alpha-\beta}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(10.26)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{How much this adds.}  The elasticity is $(\alpha+\beta)/(1-\alpha-\beta)$ where the
one-capital model of Chapter 8 gave $\alpha/(1-\alpha)$:
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
(\alpha,\beta) & (1/3,0) & (1/3,1/6) & (1/3,1/3)\\
\text{exponent} & 0.50 & 1.00 & 2.00\\
\text{eightfold distortion} & 2.83 & 8.00 & 64.00
\end{array}
\]
Adding a human capital share equal to the physical capital share quadruples the exponent, and the
income ratio a given distortion can generate rises from under threefold to sixty-fourfold.

\rmk{The mechanism deserves scepticism, and the text supplies it.  The large number comes entirely
from an extremely elastic human capital response: the model has human capital adjusting to
after-tax returns exactly as physical capital does, so a tax on investment compounds across both
margins.  Whether schooling decisions really respond to corporate taxes or to corruption in that
way is doubtful, and the exercise's arithmetic is the same arithmetic that makes the
Mankiw--Romer--Weil accounting attribute so much to human capital --- which Chapter 3 found the
evidence does not support.  What the calculation establishes is a logical possibility, not a
measurement.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.12}{}
\emph{(a) The consumer.}  The representative household owns the two capital stocks and rents them
to firms at $R_k(t)$ and $R_h(t)$, and supplies its unit of raw labor at the wage $w(t)$.  It
solves
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}u\bigl(c(t)\bigr)\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
c(t)+i_k(t)+i_h(t)\le R_k(t)k(t)+R_h(t)h(t)+w(t),
\]
\[
\dt k(t)=i_k(t)-\delta_kk(t),
\qquad
\dt h(t)=i_h(t)-\delta_hh(t),
\qquad
k(0),h(0)>0\ \text{given},
\]
together with the no-Ponzi conditions
$\lim_{t\to\infty}k(t)e^{-\int_0^t(R_k-\delta_k)}\ge0$ and the analogue in $h$.  Prices are taken
as given.

\emph{(b) Equilibrium.}  A competitive equilibrium is a path
\[
\bigl[c(t),i_k(t),i_h(t),k(t),h(t),R_k(t),R_h(t),w(t)\bigr]_{t=0}^{\infty}
\]
such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item the household's path solves the problem in (a) given prices;
\item firms maximize: with $F(K,H,L)$ constant returns to scale and $L=1$,
      \[
      R_k(t)=f_k\bigl(k(t),h(t)\bigr),\quad
      R_h(t)=f_h\bigl(k(t),h(t)\bigr),\quad
      w(t)=f\bigl(k(t),h(t)\bigr)-k(t)f_k-h(t)f_h ;
      \]
\item markets clear: the factor markets by (2), and the goods market by
      $c(t)+i_k(t)+i_h(t)=f(k(t),h(t))$, which follows from (2) and Euler's theorem.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{(c) Characterization.}  The household's current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H=u\bigl(R_kk+R_hh+w-i_k-i_h\bigr)+\mu_k(i_k-\delta_kk)+\mu_h(i_h-\delta_hh),
\]
with $R_k,R_h,w$ exogenous to the household.  The conditions in the two investments give
$\mu_k=\mu_h=u'(c)$ again, and the two costate equations give
\[
\frac{\dt\mu_k}{\mu_k}=\rho+\delta_k-R_k(t),
\qquad
\frac{\dt\mu_h}{\mu_h}=\rho+\delta_h-R_h(t).
\]
Equality of the costates forces the \emph{no-arbitrage condition}
\[
R_k(t)-\delta_k=R_h(t)-\delta_h\ \equiv\ r(t),
\]
and substituting the firms' conditions from (2) turns this into
$f_k(k,h)-f_h(k,h)=\delta_k-\delta_h$, which is \bk{(10.25)}; so $h(t)=\xi(k(t))$ in equilibrium
too.  The Euler equation is
\[
\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c)}\bigl[r(t)-\rho\bigr]
=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c)}\bigl[f_k\bigl(k,\xi(k)\bigr)-\delta_k-\rho\bigr] ,
\]
the resource constraint reproduces the second equation of \bk{Proposition 10.1}, and the
transversality conditions are the ones derived in Exercise 10.10.

Every equilibrium condition is therefore identical to an optimality condition of the planner's
problem, and conversely; since both problems have a unique solution given $k(0)$, the competitive
equilibrium and the optimal growth path coincide. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why they coincide, and what is doing the work.}  There are no externalities, technology has
constant returns, markets are complete and the household is a price taker, so the First and Second
Welfare Theorems of Chapter 5 apply.  What is worth noticing is the interpretation of
\bk{(10.25)}: in the planner's problem it is a first-order condition, an instruction to keep the
two capitals in balance; in the market it is a \emph{no-arbitrage condition}, forced by the fact
that both assets are produced from the same good at the same price and can be converted at will.
The balance the planner chooses is the balance arbitrage imposes.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.13}{}
Let $Y(t)=F\bigl(K(t),H(t),A(t)L\bigr)$ with $A(t)=A(0)e^{gt}$, $L=1$ and $F$ constant returns to
scale in its three arguments; preferences are CRRA with curvature $\theta$.

\emph{(a) Equilibrium} is defined exactly as in Exercise 10.12, with the firm's conditions
\[
R_k(t)=F_K,\qquad R_h(t)=F_H,\qquad w(t)=F_L\cdot A(t) ,
\]
where $w$ is now the wage per unit of raw labor and grows over time.

\emph{(b) Transformed variables.}  Because progress is labor augmenting and $F$ has constant
returns, the natural normalization is by effective labor:
\[
\wh k\equiv\frac{K}{AL},\qquad \wh h\equiv\frac{H}{AL},\qquad \wh c\equiv\frac{c}{A},
\qquad f(\wh k,\wh h)\equiv F(\wh k,\wh h,1),
\]
and these are the variables constant in a steady state; $c$, $k$, $h$ and $y$ all grow at $g$.
The accumulation equations become
\[
\dt{\wh k}=\wh\imath_k-(\delta_k+g)\wh k,
\qquad
\dt{\wh h}=\wh\imath_h-(\delta_h+g)\wh h,
\]
effective labor growing at $g$ adding to the effective depreciation rate of both stocks.

\emph{(c) Steady state and dynamics.}  The two investment conditions again give
$\mu_k=\mu_h$, and the two costate equations again give
\[
f_k(\wh k,\wh h)-\delta_k=f_h(\wh k,\wh h)-\delta_h ,
\]
which is \bk{(10.25)} unchanged: the balance locus $\wh h=\xi(\wh k)$ is the \emph{same function}
as without technological progress, since $g$ enters both stocks symmetrically.  The Euler equation
with CRRA preferences is
\[
\frac{\dt{\wh c}}{\wh c}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl[f_k\bigl(\wh k,\xi(\wh k)\bigr)-\delta_k-\rho-\theta g\Bigr],
\]
so the steady state is
\[
\boxed{\ f_k\bigl(\wh k^{*},\xi(\wh k^{*})\bigr)=\rho+\delta_k+\theta g,
\qquad
f_h\bigl(\wh k^{*},\wh h^{*}\bigr)=\rho+\delta_h+\theta g,
\qquad \wh h^{*}=\xi(\wh k^{*}),\ }
\]
the modified golden rule of Chapter 8 applied to each capital stock, and
\[
\dt{\wh k}=\frac{1}{1+\xi'(\wh k)}
\Bigl[f\bigl(\wh k,\xi(\wh k)\bigr)-(\delta_h+g)\xi(\wh k)-(\delta_k+g)\wh k-\wh c\Bigr] .
\]
This is a planar system in $(\wh k,\wh c)$ with exactly the structure of \bk{Proposition 8.7}: a
unique steady state, saddle-path stable, with monotone convergence along the stable arm.  A
higher $g$ raises the required marginal product $\rho+\delta_k+\theta g$ and therefore \emph{lowers}
$\wh k^{*}$ and $\wh h^{*}$; the technology-adjusted levels fall while the growth rates rise.
Assumption 4 --- $\rho>(1-\theta)g$ with $n=0$ --- is again needed for finite utility.

\emph{(d) Why human capital accumulates faster.}  Two observations combine.

First, along the balanced growth path $\wh h=H/(AL)$ is constant, so the \emph{stock} of human
capital grows at exactly the rate $g$.  A higher $g$ therefore means permanently faster human
capital accumulation, not just faster output growth.

Second, the mechanism is complementarity.  Human capital is complementary with effective labor,
$F_{HA}>0$; as $A$ rises, the marginal product of a given stock of human capital rises with it,
and by \bk{(10.25)} the return to human capital must remain tied to the return to physical
capital.  So the return to human capital investment rises one for one with the rate of
technological progress, and the household responds by accumulating $H$ at the rate $g$.  Since
$\wh h=\xi(\wh k)$ is a fixed relation, human capital tracks physical capital exactly: anything
that raises the growth rate of $K$ raises that of $H$ by the same amount.  With
$f=\wh k^{\alpha}\wh h^{\beta}$ and $\delta_k=\delta_h$ the ratio is literally constant,
$\wh h^{*}/\wh k^{*}=\beta/\alpha$, regardless of $g$.

\rmk{This is the model's answer to a recurring empirical question --- whether skill-biased
technological change should raise or lower the incentive to invest in skills.  Here the answer is
unambiguous, because progress is factor-neutral in the relevant sense and the two capitals are
tied by \bk{(10.25)}.  Models in which technology and skills are chosen jointly, and in which the
\emph{direction} of technological change responds to relative supplies, are the subject of Chapter
15, and there the answer is considerably less simple.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.15}{}
By constant returns to scale and Euler's theorem,
\[
F_K(K,H)K+F_H(K,H)H=F(K,H)\qquad\text{for all }K,H>0 .
\]
Differentiate with respect to $K$:
\[
F_{KK}K+F_K+F_{HK}H=F_K
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
F_{KK}K+F_{KH}H=0 ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ F_{KH}=-\frac{K}{H}F_{KK}\ \ge\ 0\ }
\]
since $F$ is concave in $K$ (Assumption 1), so $F_{KK}\le0$, and $K,H>0$.  If $F$ is
\emph{strictly} concave in $K$ the inequality is strict. $\blacksquare$

The symmetric computation, differentiating Euler's identity with respect to $H$, gives
$F_{KH}=-\tfrac{H}{K}F_{HH}$, and multiplying the two,
\[
F_{KH}^{2}=F_{KK}F_{HH}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\det\begin{pmatrix}F_{KK}&F_{KH}\\F_{KH}&F_{HH}\end{pmatrix}=0 .
\]
The Hessian of a constant-returns production function is always singular, with null direction
$(K,H)$ --- the direction in which the function is linear.

\emph{Verification.}  For the CES $F=(K^{\rho}+H^{\rho})^{1/\rho}$ with $\rho=-1/2$ (elasticity of
substitution $2/3$), the program computes $F_{KK}$ and $F_{KH}$ by finite differences:
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
(K,H) & (1,1) & (1,3) & (4,1)\\
F_{KK} & -0.093750 & -0.139899 & -0.009260\\
F_{KH} & +0.093750 & +0.046634 & +0.037037\\
-(K/H)F_{KK} & +0.093750 & +0.046633 & +0.037039
\end{array}
\]

\emph{What this means.}  With exactly two factors and constant returns, capital and human capital
\emph{cannot} be substitutes in the sense $F_{KH}<0$; complementarity is not an assumption but a
theorem.  The intuition is that constant returns tie the two marginal products together: doubling
both factors doubles output and leaves both marginal products unchanged, so if raising $K$ alone
lowers $F_K$, raising $H$ must raise it.  Note that this argument is special to two factors ---
with three or more, a pair of factors can be substitutes while constant returns still hold, which
is why the production function $F(K,H,L)$ of Section 10.4 needs $f_{kh}>0$ as a separate
assumption.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.16}{}
As in Exercise 9.4, the accounting question is who holds the undepreciated capital.  Each adult
of generation $t$ owns $b_i(t-1)$, rents it to firms, and at the end of the period receives the
rental $f'(\kappa(t))b_i(t-1)$ together with the $(1-\delta)b_i(t-1)$ that has not depreciated.
His gross return is therefore
\[
R(t)=f'\bigl(\kappa(t)\bigr)+1-\delta ,
\]
replacing \bk{(10.32)}, while the wage per unit of human capital is unchanged,
\[
w(t)=f\bigl(\kappa(t)\bigr)-\kappa(t)f'\bigl(\kappa(t)\bigr).
\]

\emph{What does not change.}  The individual's problem is still to maximize
$m_i(t)-\gamma(h_i(t)/a)$ by Exercise 10.17, and $m_i(t)=w(t)h_i(t)+R(t)b_i(t-1)$ is linear in
$h_i$ with coefficient $w(t)$, which does not involve $\delta$.  So the human capital condition
\bk{(10.35)} and its solution \bk{(10.36)},
\[
aw(t)=\gamma'\Bigl(\frac{h_i(t)}{a}\Bigr),
\qquad
h_i(t)=h(t)\equiv a\,\gamma'^{-1}\Bigl(a\bigl[f(\kappa(t))-\kappa(t)f'(\kappa(t))\bigr]\Bigr),
\]
are word for word the same.  Every individual still chooses the same human capital, and it still
depends on nothing but the effective capital-labor ratio: the two substantive conclusions of
Section 10.5 survive untouched.

\emph{What changes, and how little.}  Bequests are still $b_i(t)=(1-\eta)m_i(t)$, so
\[
K(t+1)=\int_{0}^{1}b_i(t)\dd i=(1-\eta)\bigl[w(t)H(t)+R(t)K(t)\bigr]
=(1-\eta)\bigl[Y(t)+(1-\delta)K(t)\bigr],
\]
using Euler's theorem $w(t)H(t)+f'(\kappa(t))K(t)=Y(t)$.  Dividing by $H(t+1)=h(t+1)$ and using
$Y(t)=f(\kappa(t))h(t)$,
\[
\kappa(t+1)=\frac{(1-\eta)\bigl[f(\kappa(t))+(1-\delta)\kappa(t)\bigr]h(t)}{h(t+1)} ,
\]
which is \bk{(10.37)} with $f$ replaced by
\[
\wt f(\kappa)\equiv f(\kappa)+(1-\delta)\kappa
\]
in the accumulation term only (the factor prices still involve $f$ itself).  The steady state is
\[
\kappa^{*}=(1-\eta)\wt f(\kappa^{*})=(1-\eta)\bigl[f(\kappa^{*})+(1-\delta)\kappa^{*}\bigr],
\]
in place of \bk{(10.38)}.  This equation has a unique positive root for exactly the same reason:
$\wt f$ is strictly concave with $\wt f(0)=0$ and $\wt f'(0)=\infty$, and
$(1-\eta)\wt f'(\kappa)\to(1-\eta)(1-\delta)<1$, so $(1-\eta)\wt f(\kappa)-\kappa$ is positive
near zero and eventually negative.  \bk{Proposition 10.2} therefore holds verbatim with $\wt f$ in
place of $f$, and the conclusion drawn from it --- that capital-skill complementarity pins down a
target ratio $\kappa^{*}$ and so rules out large imbalances --- is unaffected.

\rmk{The exercise says the dynamics ``remain unchanged''; strictly they do not --- the
steady-state condition acquires the term $(1-\delta)\kappa^{*}$, and $\kappa^{*}$ is larger for
$\delta<1$ than for $\delta=1$, since more capital survives each period.  What is unchanged is
everything the section uses: the human capital decision, its independence of the wealth
distribution, the uniqueness of $\kappa^{*}$, and the balance result.  Setting $\delta=1$ is a
normalization that shortens \bk{(10.38)}, not a substantive restriction.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.17}{}
Preferences \bk{(10.28)} are
\[
\eta^{-\eta}(1-\eta)^{-(1-\eta)}c_i(t)^{\eta}b_i(t)^{1-\eta}-\gamma\Bigl(\frac{h_i(t)}{a}\Bigr),
\]
and the budget constraint \bk{(10.29)} is $c_i(t)+b_i(t)\le m_i(t)$.

\emph{(10.33).}  The effort term does not involve $c_i$ or $b_i$, so the consumption-bequest
split solves $\max c^{\eta}b^{1-\eta}$ subject to $c+b=m$ (the constraint binds since the
objective is strictly increasing).  Taking logs and differentiating,
\[
\frac{\eta}{c}=\frac{1-\eta}{b}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
b=\frac{1-\eta}{\eta}c,
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
c\Bigl(1+\frac{1-\eta}{\eta}\Bigr)=\frac{c}{\eta}=m ,
\]
giving
\[
c_i(t)=\eta\,m_i(t),
\qquad
b_i(t)=(1-\eta)\,m_i(t),
\]
which is \bk{(10.33)}: constant shares, as always with Cobb--Douglas preferences.

\emph{(10.34).}  Substituting back,
\[
\eta^{-\eta}(1-\eta)^{-(1-\eta)}\bigl(\eta m\bigr)^{\eta}\bigl((1-\eta)m\bigr)^{1-\eta}
=\eta^{-\eta}(1-\eta)^{-(1-\eta)}\eta^{\eta}(1-\eta)^{1-\eta}m^{\eta+(1-\eta)}
=m ,
\]
every constant cancelling against the normalizing factor.  Hence the indirect utility function is
\[
\boxed{\ m_i(t)-\gamma\Bigl(\frac{h_i(t)}{a}\Bigr)\ }
\]
which is \bk{(10.34)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The point of the normalization.}  The factor $\eta^{-\eta}(1-\eta)^{-(1-\eta)}$ is chosen so
that indirect utility is exactly \emph{linear} in income, with a coefficient of one.  That makes
the human capital decision a pure income-maximization problem: from \bk{(10.34)} the individual
chooses $h_i$ to maximize $w(t)h_i-\gamma(h_i/a)$, which involves neither his inherited wealth
$b_i(t-1)$ nor any preference parameter other than $\gamma$.  Consequently
\begin{itemize}
\item every individual chooses the \emph{same} human capital, \bk{(10.36)}, however unequal their
      bequests --- there are no income effects in schooling;
\item aggregate dynamics do not depend on the distribution of wealth, which is what makes
      \bk{(10.37)} a scalar difference equation;
\item the human capital choice ``separates from'' the consumption-bequest choice.
\end{itemize}
The last point is \bk{Theorem 10.1} in a two-period discrete-time setting: here it is delivered not
by perfect capital markets but by quasi-linear preferences, which serve the same purpose of making
the marginal value of income independent of how much of it there is.  Exercise 10.20 in
Section 9.6 --- general $u_1(c)+u_2(b)$ --- is where dropping this structure starts to matter.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{10.19}{}
\emph{Closing the economy.}  Suppose there are two groups, of measures $\pi$ and $1-\pi$, with
abilities $a_1$ and $a_2$.  Everything in Section 10.6 is unchanged except that the interest rate
no longer comes from world markets: capital per firm is whatever last period's bequests supply,
\[
\wh k(t)=\int_{0}^{1}b_i(t-1)\dd i=K(t),
\]
and $R(t)$ adjusts to clear the capital market, $R(t)=(1-\lambda)\int_0^1F_k(\wh k(t),h_i(t))\dd i$.
The essential change is that $\wh k(t)$ is \emph{predetermined}: firms invest before they know
which worker they will meet, and the aggregate they can invest was fixed a period earlier.

Given $\wh k(t)$, each worker chooses human capital from
\[
\lambda a_i\,F_h\bigl(\wh k(t),h_i\bigr)=\gamma'\Bigl(\frac{h_i}{a_i}\Bigr),
\tag{W}
\]
which defines $\wh h_i(\wh k(t);a_i)$.  Differentiating (W) in $\wh k$ and in $a_i$,
\[
\frac{\partial\wh h_i}{\partial\wh k}=\frac{\lambda a_iF_{hk}}{\gamma''/a_i-\lambda a_iF_{hh}}>0 ,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\wh h_i}{\partial a_i}=\frac{\lambda F_h+\gamma''h_i/a_i^{2}}{\gamma''/a_i-\lambda a_iF_{hh}}>0 ,
\]
both denominators positive because $\gamma$ is strictly convex and $F$ concave in $h$, and both
numerators positive because $F_{hk}\ge0$ by Exercise 10.15.  With Cobb--Douglas bequests as in
Section 10.5, total income equals total output and
\[
\wh k(t+1)=(1-\eta)\,\Phi\bigl(\wh k(t)\bigr),
\qquad
\Phi(\wh k)\equiv\int_{0}^{1}F\bigl(\wh k,\wh h_i(\wh k)\bigr)\dd i=Y(t) .
\tag{K}
\]

\emph{The externality.}  Raise $a_1$.  At the impact date $t=0$:
\begin{itemize}
\item group 1's human capital rises, by $\partial\wh h_1/\partial a_1>0$;
\item current output $Y(0)=\Phi(\wh k(0))$ rises, since $F$ is increasing in $h$;
\item group 2's human capital is \emph{unchanged}, because $\wh h_2$ depends on $a_2$ and on
      $\wh k(0)$, and $\wh k(0)$ is predetermined.
\end{itemize}
From $t=1$ on, by (K), the higher output raises bequests and therefore $\wh k(1)$; the higher
capital per firm raises $\wh h_i$ for \emph{every} $i$, including group 2, which raises output
again, and so on.  The long-run effect is
\[
\frac{\dd\wh k^{*}}{\dd a_1}
=\frac{(1-\eta)\,\partial\Phi/\partial a_1}{1-(1-\eta)\Phi'(\wh k^{*})}\ >\ 0,
\qquad
\Phi'(\wh k)=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl[F_k+F_h\frac{\partial\wh h_i}{\partial\wh k}\Bigr]\dd i>\int_0^1F_k\dd i,
\]
with the multiplier $1/[1-(1-\eta)\Phi'(\wh k^{*})]>1$ under the stability condition
$(1-\eta)\Phi'(\wh k^{*})<1$.  Group 2's steady-state human capital rises by
$(\partial\wh h_2/\partial\wh k)\,\dd\wh k^{*}/\dd a_1>0$.

\emph{An explicit example.}  Take $F(k,h)=k^{1/3}h^{2/3}$, $\gamma(e)=e^{3}/3$, $\lambda=1/2$,
$\eta=0.4$, $\pi=1/2$, $a_1=a_2=1$.  Then (W) solves in closed form,
$\wh h_i=\bigl[\tfrac{2}{3}\lambda a_i^{3}\wh k^{1/3}\bigr]^{3/7}\propto a_i^{9/7}\wh k^{1/7}$,
and (K) becomes $\wh k(t+1)=(1-\eta)C\wh k(t)^{3/7}$, a contraction with a unique stable steady
state.  Raising $a_1$ by $10\%$ gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccccccc}
t & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 5 & \infty\\
\wh k(t) & 0.23616 & 0.24621 & 0.25065 & 0.25257 & 0.25376 & 0.25403\\
\wh h_2(t)\ \text{gain} & 0.000\% & 0.597\% & 0.854\% & 0.965\% & 1.032\% & 1.047\%
\end{array}
\]
The closed forms are $\dd\log\wh k^{*}/\dd\log a_1=0.750$ and
$\dd\log\wh h_2^{*}/\dd\log a_1=0.107$, which the numerical experiment reproduces ($0.765$ and
$0.109$ for a discrete $10\%$ change).  Group 2 ends up with over $1\%$ more human capital,
without any change in its own ability, preferences or constraints.  The right panel of
Figure~\ref{fig:ch10} shows the accumulation map (K) shifting up and the economy climbing to the
new steady state.

\emph{Why this is different from the open economy.}  In the open economy of \bk{Proposition 10.3},
$\wh k$ is pinned down by $(1-\lambda)\int F_k(\wh k,\wh h_i(\wh k))\dd i=R^{*}$, so a rise in
$a_1$ does raise $\wh k$ and hence $\wh h_2$ --- but instantaneously, and financed from abroad.
Here the adjustment must be financed out of the economy's own saving, so it takes time, arrives
only from the next period, and compounds: higher output this period buys more capital next
period, which raises everyone's human capital, which raises output again.  That is the sense in
which the externality is \emph{dynamic}.

\rmk{The externality exists at all only because of the labor market friction.  With competitive
labor markets (Sections 10.4 and 10.5) each worker is paid his marginal product and internalizes
the full return to his human capital, so raising one group's ability changes the others' decisions
only through factor prices in the usual, second-order way.  Here random matching plus a fixed
sharing rule $\lambda$ means the return each worker captures depends on the capital \emph{other}
people's investments have called forth; complementarity then turns a private improvement into a
public one.  This is the mechanism Chapter 21 develops into a full theory of human capital
externalities and virtuous cycles.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{11}{First-Generation Models of Endogenous Growth}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The thirteen exercises of Chapter 11 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 11.1--11.3,
11.5--11.7, 11.9--11.13, 11.19 and 11.20.  The manual solves 11.4, 11.8, 11.14--11.18 and 11.21.
Notation follows the book: $A$ is the productivity of the linear technology, $g^{*}$ the
equilibrium growth rate, $\theta$ the inverse of the intertemporal elasticity of substitution,
$\kappa$ the share of capital in the investment sector of Section 11.3, and
$\wt f(L)\equiv F(1,BL)$ the normalized production function of the Romer model of Section 11.4.
The numerical parts are computed by \texttt{code/ch11.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{11.1}{}
Write $\lambda\equiv A-\delta-n$ and $g\equiv\theta^{-1}(A-\delta-\rho)$, so that \bk{(11.13)} is
the first-order linear nonautonomous equation
\[
\dt k(t)=\lambda k(t)-c(0)e^{gt} .
\]
Multiply by the integrating factor $e^{-\lambda t}$:
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\Bigl[k(t)e^{-\lambda t}\Bigr]=-c(0)e^{(g-\lambda)t} ,
\]
and integrate.  Provided $g\neq\lambda$,
\[
k(t)e^{-\lambda t}=\kappa-\frac{c(0)}{g-\lambda}e^{(g-\lambda)t}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
k(t)=\kappa e^{\lambda t}+\frac{c(0)}{\lambda-g}e^{gt} ,
\]
where $\kappa$ is a constant of integration.  Finally,
\[
\lambda-g=(A-\delta-n)-\frac{A-\delta-\rho}{\theta}
=\frac{(A-\delta)(\theta-1)+\rho}{\theta}-n
=(A-\delta)(\theta-1)\theta^{-1}+\rho\theta^{-1}-n ,
\]
so that
\[
k(t)=\kappa e^{(A-\delta-n)t}
+\bigl[(A-\delta)(\theta-1)\theta^{-1}+\rho\theta^{-1}-n\bigr]^{-1}
\Bigl[c(0)\exp\bigl(\theta^{-1}(A-\delta-\rho)t\bigr)\Bigr] ,
\]
which is \bk{(11.14)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The sign of $\lambda-g$.}  The second inequality in \bk{(11.12)} states
$\rho+\delta>(1-\theta)(A-\delta)+\theta n+\delta$, that is $\rho>(1-\theta)(A-\delta)+\theta n$;
dividing by $\theta>0$ gives exactly $\lambda-g>0$.  (This is also what makes the household's
utility finite: along the equilibrium path consumption grows at $g$, and
$\int_0^\infty e^{-(\rho-n)t}c(t)^{1-\theta}\dd t$ converges if and only if $\rho-n>(1-\theta)g$,
which rearranges to the same inequality.)

\emph{Why the constant vanishes.}  Substituting \bk{(11.14)} into the transversality condition
\bk{(11.10)}, and noting that $k(t)e^{-\lambda t}=\kappa+\frac{c(0)}{\lambda-g}e^{-(\lambda-g)t}$,
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}k(t)e^{-\lambda t}=\kappa ,
\]
because $\lambda-g>0$ kills the second term.  The transversality condition therefore forces
$\kappa=0$, leaving
\[
k(t)=\frac{c(0)}{\lambda-g}e^{gt}=k(0)e^{gt},
\qquad
c(0)=(\lambda-g)k(0),
\]
which are \bk{(11.15)} and \bk{(11.16)}.  Capital, consumption and output all grow at $g$ from the
very first instant: there are no transitional dynamics.

\rmk{The knife-edge case $g=\lambda$ is excluded by \bk{(11.12)}, but it is worth knowing what it
looks like: the particular solution is then $-c(0)te^{\lambda t}$ and
$k(t)=[\kappa-c(0)t]e^{\lambda t}$, which is eventually negative for any $\kappa$.  The condition
$\lambda>g$ is thus not a technicality --- it is what makes a positive capital stock consistent
with consumption growing at the Euler rate forever.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.2}{}
Two proofs; the direct one is more informative.

\emph{The optimal growth problem.}  The planner maximizes \bk{(11.1)} subject to the resource
constraint $\dt k=(A-\delta-n)k-c$ and $k(0)>0$.  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H(k,c,\mu)=\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu\bigl[(A-\delta-n)k-c\bigr] ,
\]
so the conditions of \bk{Theorem 7.13} are
\[
c(t)^{-\theta}=\mu(t),
\qquad
\mu(t)(A-\delta-n)=(\rho-n)\mu(t)-\dt\mu(t),
\qquad
\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)k(t)=0 .
\]
The second gives $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-(A-\delta)$, and since $\mu=c^{-\theta}$ implies
$\dt\mu/\mu=-\theta\dt c/c$,
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(A-\delta-\rho\bigr),
\]
which is \bk{(11.9)}.  For the transversality condition, substitute
$\mu(t)=c(0)^{-\theta}e^{-(A-\delta-\rho)t}$:
\[
e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)k(t)=c(0)^{-\theta}k(t)e^{-(A-\delta-n)t} ,
\]
so the condition is exactly \bk{(11.10)}.  Every condition of the planner's problem therefore
coincides with an equilibrium condition.

\emph{Sufficiency and uniqueness.}  $\wh H$ is the sum of a strictly concave function of $c$ and a
function linear in $(k,c)$, hence concave in $(k,c)$ and strictly concave in $c$; and
$\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)k(t)\ge0$ for every feasible path because $\mu>0$ and
$k\ge0$.  \bk{Theorem 7.14} therefore applies: the conditions above are sufficient, and by strict
concavity in $c$ the optimum is unique.  Since the competitive equilibrium of
\bk{Proposition 11.1} satisfies them, it \emph{is} the optimum, and in particular it is Pareto
optimal. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The Welfare Theorem route.}  Alternatively, the economy has a representative household, all
markets are competitive and there are no externalities, so \bk{Theorem 5.6} applies provided the
value of the aggregate endowment at equilibrium prices is finite.  Here the household's only
endowment is its initial capital $k(0)$ --- labor earns nothing, since $Y=AK$ does not use labor
--- so that value is $k(0)<\infty$, trivially.  Hence the equilibrium is Pareto optimal.

\rmk{It is worth being explicit about why sustained endogenous growth and Pareto optimality
coexist here, when they will not in Section 11.4.  Growth in the $AK$ model comes from the
\emph{absence of diminishing returns}, not from any externality: each firm's private marginal
product of capital, $A$, is also its social marginal product.  In the Romer model the two differ,
and \bk{Proposition 11.6} shows the equilibrium then grows too slowly.  Endogenous growth is not,
in itself, a source of inefficiency.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.3}{}
\emph{(a) Equilibrium.}  With $Y(t)=AK(t)+BL(t)$, both factors have constant marginal products,
so competitive pricing gives
\[
R(t)=A,\qquad r(t)=r=A-\delta,\qquad w(t)=w=B
\qquad\text{for all }t .
\]
A competitive equilibrium is a path $[c(t),k(t),w(t),R(t)]_{t=0}^{\infty}$ such that the
representative household maximizes $\int_0^\infty e^{-(\rho-n)t}\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t$
subject to $\dt a=(r-n)a+w-c$ and the no-Ponzi condition, given $k(0)$; factor prices are as
above; and $a(t)=k(t)$.

\emph{(b) Characterization.}  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H(a,c,\mu)=\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu\bigl[(r-n)a+w-c\bigr] ,
\]
giving $c^{-\theta}=\mu$, $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-r$ and the transversality condition.  Hence
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\theta}(A-\delta-\rho)\equiv g ,
\]
a constant, exactly as in the baseline $AK$ model.  The capital accumulation equation is now
\[
\dt k(t)=\lambda k(t)+B-c(0)e^{gt},
\qquad \lambda\equiv A-\delta-n ,
\]
which differs from \bk{(11.13)} by the constant $B$ --- labor income.  Solving as in Exercise 11.1,
\[
k(t)=\kappa e^{\lambda t}-\frac{B}{\lambda}+\frac{c(0)}{\lambda-g}e^{gt},
\]
and the transversality condition again forces $\kappa=0$, so that
\[
\boxed{\ k(t)+\frac{B}{\lambda}=\Bigl[k(0)+\frac{B}{\lambda}\Bigr]e^{gt},
\qquad
c(0)=(\lambda-g)\Bigl[k(0)+\frac{B}{\lambda}\Bigr] .\ }
\]
The object that grows at the constant rate $g$ is not capital but \emph{total wealth}: physical
capital plus $B/\lambda$, the present value of the constant labor income stream discounted at the
effective rate $\lambda=r-n$.

\emph{Transitional dynamics.}  Since $k(t)$ itself is total wealth minus a constant,
\[
\frac{\dt k(t)}{k(t)}=g\Bigl[1+\frac{B}{\lambda k(t)}\Bigr]\ >\ g ,
\]
which is strictly decreasing in $k$ and tends to $g$ only as $t\to\infty$.  Capital therefore
grows \emph{faster} than consumption at every finite date, at a rate that falls monotonically
toward $g$: the economy has nontrivial transitional dynamics even though consumption growth is
constant.  Output $y=Ak+B$ behaves the same way,
\[
\frac{\dt y}{y}=\frac{A\dt k}{Ak+B}=g\,\frac{A(k+B/\lambda)}{Ak+B}\ >\ g
\quad\text{(because $A/\lambda>1$)},
\]
again falling monotonically to $g$.  With $A=0.15$, $B=0.30$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$,
$n=0.01$, $\theta=3$ and $k(0)=1$ the program gives $g=0.02667$ and
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
t & 0 & 25 & 50 & 100 & 200\\
\dt k/k & 0.1156 & 0.0441 & 0.0334 & 0.0282 & 0.0268\\
\dt y/y & 0.0385 & 0.0317 & 0.0290 & 0.0272 & 0.0267
\end{array}
\]

\emph{(c) The labor share.}  Labor income is $BL$ and output is $AK+BL$, so
\[
\frac{w(t)L(t)}{Y(t)}=\frac{B}{Ak(t)+B} ,
\]
which is strictly decreasing in $k$ and, since $k(t)\to\infty$, falls monotonically to zero.  In
the calibration above it goes from $0.667$ at $t=0$ to $0.132$ at $t=50$ and $0.0022$ at
$t=200$ (Figure~\ref{fig:ch11}, right panel).  The capital share rises to one.  This is the
baseline $AK$ model's counterfactual prediction arriving more slowly rather than being avoided:
adding raw labor delays the outcome without changing it, because labor is a factor in fixed
supply whose marginal product does not grow.

\emph{(d) An unanticipated rise in $B$.}  The growth rate $g$ depends only on $A$, $\delta$,
$\rho$ and $\theta$, so it is unchanged --- permanently.  What changes is the level.  Human
wealth $B/\lambda$ jumps up, so consumption jumps up immediately by
$(\lambda-g)\Delta B/\lambda$; capital is predetermined and does not jump, but its subsequent
growth rate $g[1+B/(\lambda k)]$ is higher at every $k$, so $k$ and $y$ rise over time relative to
the old path.  The labor share jumps up and then resumes its decline to zero.  So the effect is a
level effect asymptotically, arriving through a temporary increase in the growth rate --- the
opposite pattern from a change in $A$, which changes the asymptotic growth rate itself.

\emph{(e) Pareto optimality.}  The planner's resource constraint is
$\dt k=(A-\delta-n)k+B-c$ and its Hamiltonian is concave in $(k,c)$, strictly so in $c$;
repeating Exercise 11.2 word for word gives the same Euler equation, the same transversality
condition and hence the same allocation.  \bk{Theorem 7.14} makes the conditions sufficient, so
the equilibrium is the unique optimum.  Equivalently, \bk{Theorem 5.6} applies because the value
of the aggregate endowment at equilibrium prices, $k(0)+B/\lambda$, is finite --- which is exactly
the condition $\lambda>g>0$ established in Exercise 11.1.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch11_endogenous_growth.pdf}
\caption{Left (Exercise 11.6): the growth rate of consumption in the neoclassical model with
$y=Ak^{\alpha}$ and log preferences.  As $\alpha\to1$ the curve flattens onto the $AK$ line
$A-\delta-\rho$ and the steady state disappears to infinity.  Right (Exercise 11.3): in the
$AK+BL$ economy the labor share falls to zero while output growth declines monotonically to $g$
--- nontrivial transitional dynamics in a model with a constant consumption growth rate.}
\label{fig:ch11}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.5}{}
\emph{The growth rate.}  With the budget constraint \bk{(11.18)},
$\dt a=((1-\tau)r-n)a+w-c$, the current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H=\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu\bigl[\bigl((1-\tau)r-n\bigr)a+w-c\bigr] ,
\]
so $c^{-\theta}=\mu$ and
\[
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=(\rho-n)-\bigl((1-\tau)r-n\bigr)=\rho-(1-\tau)r .
\]
Using $\dt\mu/\mu=-\theta\dt c/c$ and $r=A-\delta$,
\[
g=\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac{(1-\tau)(A-\delta)-\rho}{\theta} ,
\]
which is \bk{(11.19)}.  The tax reduces growth one for one with the after-tax return, and
$\dd g/\dd\tau=-(A-\delta)/\theta<0$.

\emph{The saving rate.}  Assume, as in Section 8.10, that the revenue is rebated lump-sum, so the
\emph{aggregate} resource constraint is still $\dt k=(A-\delta-n)k-c$.  Solving it as in Exercise
11.1 with $g$ now given by \bk{(11.19)}, the transversality condition again eliminates the
homogeneous solution and $k$ grows at $g$.  The saving rate is therefore
\[
s=\frac{\dt K+\delta K}{Y}=\frac{\dt k/k+n+\delta}{A}
=\frac{1}{A}\Bigl[\frac{(1-\tau)(A-\delta)-\rho}{\theta}+n+\delta\Bigr] .
\]
Multiplying numerator and denominator by $\theta$ and collecting the terms in $\delta$,
\[
\theta A s=(1-\tau)A-(1-\tau)\delta-\rho+\theta n+\theta\delta
=(1-\tau)A-\rho+\theta n-\bigl(1-\tau-\theta\bigr)\delta ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ s=\frac{(1-\tau)A-\rho+\theta n-(1-\tau-\theta)\delta}{\theta A}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(11.20)}; setting $\tau=0$ recovers \bk{(11.17)}.  Differentiating,
\[
\frac{\partial s}{\partial\tau}=\frac{-A+\delta}{\theta A}=-\frac{A-\delta}{\theta A}<0
\qquad\text{provided }A>\delta ,
\]
as the text states.

\emph{What is new relative to Chapter 8.}  There the saving rate was also endogenous, but a
capital tax changed only the \emph{level} of the steady state.  Here the saving rate is constant
\emph{and} the growth rate depends on it, so a permanent difference in $\tau$ becomes a permanent
difference in growth --- and hence an unbounded difference in income.  Exercise 11.7 puts numbers
on this.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.6}{}
\emph{The equilibrium.}  With $\theta=1$ the equilibrium is the planar system
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\alpha A k(t)^{\alpha-1}-\delta-\rho,
\qquad
\dt k(t)=Ak(t)^{\alpha}-c(t)-(n+\delta)k(t),
\]
with $k(0)>0$ given and the transversality condition of Chapter 8.  There is a unique steady
state,
\[
\alpha A(k^{*})^{\alpha-1}=\rho+\delta
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
k^{*}=\Bigl[\frac{\alpha A}{\rho+\delta}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},
\qquad
c^{*}=A(k^{*})^{\alpha}-(n+\delta)k^{*},
\]
saddle-path stable with monotone convergence along the stable arm (\bk{Proposition 8.7} with
$\theta=1$, $g=0$).

\emph{The limit $\alpha\to1$.}  Three things happen at once.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The Euler equation becomes the $AK$ Euler equation.}  For any fixed $k$,
      \[
      \alpha Ak^{\alpha-1}-\delta-\rho\ \longrightarrow\ A-\delta-\rho ,
      \]
      which is \bk{(11.9)} with $\theta=1$: the growth rate becomes independent of $k$.
\item \emph{The steady state runs off to infinity.}  Provided $A>\rho+\delta$, the base
      $\alpha A/(\rho+\delta)$ exceeds one for $\alpha$ close to one while the exponent
      $1/(1-\alpha)$ diverges, so $k^{*}\to\infty$: there is no finite point at which
      accumulation stops.
\item \emph{Convergence becomes infinitely slow.}  The stable eigenvalue of the linearized
      system is $\xi_1=\tfrac12\bigl[\operatorname{tr}J-\sqrt{(\operatorname{tr}J)^{2}-4\det J}\bigr]$
      with $\operatorname{tr}J=\rho-n$ and, using $\alpha A(k^{*})^{\alpha-1}=\rho+\delta$ to
      eliminate $k^{*}$,
      \[
      \det J=c^{*}f''(k^{*})=(\alpha-1)(\rho+\delta)
      \Bigl[\frac{\rho+\delta}{\alpha}-(n+\delta)\Bigr]\ \longrightarrow\ 0 ,
      \]
      so $\xi_1\to0$ and the half-life $\log2/|\xi_1|\to\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
With $A=0.15$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$, $n=0$ the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
\alpha & 1/3 & 0.6 & 0.9 & 0.99 & 0.9999\\
\log_{10}k^{*} & -0.22 & 0.27 & 2.85 & 32.66 & 3309.50\\
\xi_1 & -0.0770 & -0.0343 & -0.0072 & -0.00070 & -0.0000070\\
\text{half-life} & 9.0 & 20.2 & 96.8 & 990 & 99{,}021\\
\dt c/c\ \text{at }k=1 & -0.020 & 0.020 & 0.065 & 0.0785 & 0.07999
\end{array}
\]
against the $AK$ growth rate $A-\delta-\rho=0.08$.  The left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch11} shows
the curve $\dt c/c$ flattening onto the $AK$ line.

\emph{Interpretation.}  The $AK$ model is not a different model but the boundary case of the
neoclassical one.  What stops growth in the neoclassical model is diminishing returns --- the
failure of $f'$ to stay bounded away from zero --- and $\alpha\to1$ is precisely the limit in
which that force disappears.  Three familiar statements are the same statement: ``the Inada
condition $\lim_{k\to\infty}f'(k)=0$ fails,'' ``the steady state is at infinity,'' and ``there are
no transitional dynamics.''

This also explains why the convergence evidence of Chapter 3 bears directly on endogenous growth.
Convergence rates of about $2\%$ a year correspond to a capital share well below one; a capital
share close to one would predict essentially no convergence at all.  The debate over whether
$\alpha$ should be measured as the narrow physical capital share ($\approx1/3$) or a broad share
including human capital ($\approx2/3$ or more) is, in this light, a debate about how close the
world is to the $AK$ boundary.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.7}{}
\emph{The arithmetic.}  By \bk{(11.19)},
\[
g(\tau)=\frac{(1-\tau)(A-\delta)-\rho}{\theta}=\frac{(1-\tau)(0.10)-0.02}{3} ,
\]
so
\[
g(0.2)=\frac{0.08-0.02}{3}=0.0200,
\qquad
g(0.4)=\frac{0.06-0.02}{3}=0.0133 ,
\]
a growth gap of $0.00667$ a year.  Starting from the same income in 1900, by 2000
\[
\frac{Y(0.2,100)}{Y(0.4,100)}=e^{0.00667\times100}=e^{2/3}=1.948 ,
\]
so the low-tax country is almost exactly twice as rich.  And the gap keeps widening without
bound:
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\text{horizon (years)} & 50 & 100 & 200 & 500\\
\text{income ratio} & 1.396 & 1.948 & 3.794 & 28.03
\end{array}
\]
The saving rates, from \bk{(11.20)}, are $0.467$ and $0.422$: a difference of four and a half
percentage points, sustained forever.

\emph{Comparison with the neoclassical model.}  The same tax difference in the model of
Chapter 8 produces only a permanent \emph{level} difference,
$\bigl[(1-\tau)/(1-\tau')\bigr]^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$, which is $1.155$ at $\alpha=1/3$ and
$1.333$ at $\alpha=1/2$.  The $AK$ model converts a bounded $15\%$ level effect into an unbounded
growth effect.

\emph{Is it plausible?}  The mechanism is coherent but the implications are hard to accept, for
four reasons.

\emph{First, the world income distribution would have to diverge without limit.}  Tax rates,
expropriation risk and other determinants of the after-tax return differ across countries by far
more than twenty percentage points, and they have done so for decades.  The model then predicts
that relative incomes should be spreading apart at an accelerating rate.  The evidence in Chapter
1 is that the postwar world income distribution has been roughly stable, with substantial mobility
but no systematic divergence of the whole distribution.

\emph{Second, the capital share must tend to one}, since $Y=AK$ leaves nothing for labor.  Factor
shares have been roughly constant, which is the first of the Kaldor facts.  Exercise 11.3 shows
that adding raw labor only postpones this problem; Section 11.2 solves it, but by making human
capital accumulate linearly, which relocates rather than removes the difficulty.

\emph{Third, the sensitivity is extreme.}  Here $\dd g/\dd\tau=-(A-\delta)/\theta=-0.033$, so each
\emph{percentage point} of capital tax costs a third of a tenth of a percentage point of growth
forever --- about $3.4\%$ of income per century.  No estimate of the effect of taxation on growth
is anywhere near this large, and cross-country growth regressions find policy variables
explaining level differences far better than they explain persistent growth differences.

\emph{Fourth, and most fundamentally, the result is an artifact of exact linearity.}  The reason
small policy differences compound forever is that the marginal product of capital never falls.
Any diminishing returns at all --- $\alpha<1$ in Exercise 11.6 --- restores a steady state and
converts the growth effect into a level effect, however slowly.  A model in which the answer flips
discontinuously at $\alpha=1$ is not a good guide to a world in which $\alpha$ is measured with
error.

\rmk{The honest conclusion is the one the text draws: the $AK$ class shows that endogenous growth
and policy-sensitive growth rates are \emph{possible} in a neoclassical environment, which was
worth establishing.  It does not show that they are what generates the observed cross-country
income distribution.  That is why Part IV turns to models in which technology is chosen
purposefully and in which the linearity that sustains growth is derived from the economics of
innovation rather than assumed.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.9}{}
\emph{The effective capital-labor ratio is constant.}  The household's conditions \bk{(11.25)} are
$\mu_a=\mu_h$, $w(t)-\delta_h=r(t)$ and $\dt c/c=\theta^{-1}(r(t)-\rho)$.  The first says that the
shadow values of a unit of physical and a unit of human capital must coincide, since both are
bought with the same good and neither is subject to a nonnegativity constraint; the second is the
no-arbitrage condition it implies, equating the net returns.  Substituting the competitive factor
prices \bk{(11.24)}, $R=f'(k)$ and $w=f(k)-kf'(k)$ with $k=K/H$, the no-arbitrage condition reads
\[
f'\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\delta_k=f\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-k(t)f'\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\delta_h .
\tag{$*$}
\]
The left side is strictly decreasing in $k$ (derivative $f''<0$) and the right side strictly
increasing (derivative $-kf''>0$), so $(*)$ has at most one solution.  It has at least one:
as $k\to0$, Assumption 2 gives $f'(k)\to\infty$ while $f(k)-kf'(k)\to0$, so the left side exceeds
the right; as $k\to\infty$, $f'(k)\to0$ while $f(k)-kf'(k)\to\infty$, so the inequality reverses.
Hence there is a unique $k^{*}$ solving \bk{(11.26)}, and
\[
k(t)=k^{*}\qquad\text{for all }t\ge0 ,
\]
\emph{including} $t=0$: the economy jumps immediately to the balanced ratio by reallocating its
initial stocks, which is feasible because investment in either capital may be negative.

\emph{Growth.}  With $k$ constant, $r(t)=r^{*}=f'(k^{*})-\delta_k$ is constant, so the Euler
equation gives the constant growth rate
\[
g^{*}=\frac{f'(k^{*})-\delta_k-\rho}{\theta} .
\]
Since $k=K/H$ is constant, $K$ and $H$ grow at a common rate; output $Y=F(K,H)=Hf(k^{*})$ grows at
that same rate; and the aggregate resource constraint
\[
\dt K+\dt H=F(K,H)-C-\delta_kK-\delta_hH
\]
then reads, after dividing by $H$ and using $K=k^{*}H$,
\[
g(1+k^{*})=f(k^{*})-\frac{C}{H}-\bigl(\delta_kk^{*}+\delta_h\bigr),
\]
so $C/H$ is constant if and only if $g$ is, and the Euler equation pins $g=g^{*}$.

\emph{No transitional dynamics and uniqueness.}  Given $g^{*}$, the accumulation equation for $H$
is linear and nonautonomous, exactly as in Exercise 11.1; its homogeneous solution is eliminated
by the transversality condition, so $H$ (and therefore $K$, $Y$ and $C$) grows at $g^{*}$ from the
first instant.  The current-value Hamiltonian is concave in states and controls and strictly
concave in $c$ (the argument of Exercise 10.8), so \bk{Theorem 7.14} makes the conditions
sufficient and the path unique.

\emph{The factor shares.}  The share of capital in national income is
\[
\frac{R(t)K(t)}{Y(t)}=\frac{f'(k^{*})k^{*}}{f(k^{*})}\ \in(0,1) ,
\]
constant over time and strictly less than one by strict concavity of $f$ with $f(0)=0$.  This is
the model's advantage over the baseline $AK$ economy.

\emph{The conditions.}  $f'(k^{*})>\rho+\delta_k$ gives $g^{*}>0$, and
$\rho+\delta_k>(1-\theta)(f'(k^{*})-\delta_k)+\delta_k$, that is $\rho>(1-\theta)(f'(k^{*})-\delta_k)$,
is equivalent to $\rho>(1-\theta)g^{*}$ --- the finiteness of utility and the transversality
condition, exactly as in Exercise 11.1. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The mechanism is worth naming.  The economy behaves like an $AK$ model because, once $k$ is
pinned at $k^{*}$, the \emph{pair} $(K,H)$ accumulates linearly: $\dt K+\dt H$ is proportional to
$K+H$.  Diminishing returns to each factor separately are perfectly consistent with constant
returns to the reproducible factors taken together, and it is the latter that sustains growth.
This is the observation the text makes at the start of Section 11.3: what matters is that the
\emph{accumulation} technology is linear, not that the production function is $AK$.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.10}{}
\emph{The planner's problem.}  With $n=0$ and $L=1$, the planner maximizes \bk{(11.1)} subject to
\[
\dt K=i_K-\delta_kK,
\qquad
\dt H=i_H-\delta_hH,
\qquad
c+i_K+i_H\le F(K,H),
\]
given $K(0),H(0)>0$.  The current-value Hamiltonian, after substituting the binding resource
constraint, is
\[
\wh H=\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu_K\bigl(i_K-\delta_kK\bigr)+\mu_H\bigl(i_H-\delta_hH\bigr),
\qquad c=F(K,H)-i_K-i_H .
\]
The conditions in the two investments give
\[
\mu_K=\mu_H=c^{-\theta}\equiv\mu ,
\]
and the two costate equations give
\[
\frac{\dt\mu_K}{\mu_K}=\rho+\delta_k-F_K(K,H),
\qquad
\frac{\dt\mu_H}{\mu_H}=\rho+\delta_h-F_H(K,H) .
\]
Equality of the costates therefore forces
\[
F_K(K,H)-\delta_k=F_H(K,H)-\delta_h .
\]
Since $F$ is homogeneous of degree one, $F_K(K,H)=f'(k)$ and $F_H(K,H)=f(k)-kf'(k)$ with
$k=K/H$, so this is exactly \bk{(11.26)} and hence $k(t)=k^{*}$ for all $t$, by the uniqueness
established in Exercise 11.9.  The Euler equation is
\[
\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl[F_K(K,H)-\delta_k-\rho\bigr]
=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl[r^{*}-\rho\bigr] ,
\]
and the two transversality conditions are those of the household's problem.

\emph{Coincidence.}  Every condition of the planner's problem is an equilibrium condition of
Section 11.2 and conversely: \bk{(11.24)} turns $F_K$ into $R$ and $F_H$ into $w$, so the
planner's no-arbitrage condition is the market's, and the planner's Euler equation is the
household's.  $\wh H$ is concave in $(K,H,i_K,i_H)$ by the argument of Exercise 10.8 --- a concave
increasing $u$ composed with the concave $F(K,H)-i_K-i_H$, plus linear terms --- so
\bk{Theorem 7.14} applies and both problems have the same unique solution.  The competitive
equilibrium is therefore the optimal growth path, and in particular Pareto optimal. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The Welfare Theorem route.}  Alternatively, the economy has a representative household,
competitive markets and no externalities, and the value of its endowment at equilibrium prices ---
$a(0)$ plus the value of $h(0)$, both finite --- is finite, so \bk{Theorem 5.6} applies.

\rmk{The comparison with Exercise 10.12 is exact and worth making.  There the same no-arbitrage
condition $F_K-\delta_k=F_H-\delta_h$ defined a \emph{locus} $h=\xi(k)$ along which the economy
travelled to a steady state; here, because $F$ is homogeneous of degree one in the two
\emph{reproducible} factors, the same condition pins down a single \emph{ratio} $k^{*}$ and the
economy grows along it forever.  The difference between a neoclassical model and an endogenous
growth model, in this family, is entirely whether the third factor --- raw labor in Chapter 10 ---
is present.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.11}{}
\emph{The result.}  In Section 11.1 the equilibrium growth rate of consumption, capital and output
per capita is
\[
g^{*}=\frac{A-\delta-\rho}{\theta} ,
\]
in which $n$ does not appear.  In Section 11.2 it is
\[
g^{*}=\frac{f'(k^{*})-\delta_k-\rho}{\theta},
\qquad
f'(k^{*})-\delta_k=f(k^{*})-k^{*}f'(k^{*})-\delta_h ,
\]
and again $n$ appears nowhere: $k^{*}=K/H$ is a ratio of two accumulated stocks, so reinstating
population growth in that section changes nothing.

\emph{Why.}  The growth rate of consumption per capita comes from the Euler equation, which
compares one number, the net return $r$, with another, the discount rate $\rho$.  In these models
$r$ is determined by technology alone --- $A-\delta$, or $f'(k^{*})-\delta_k$ --- because returns
to the reproducible factors do not diminish.  Population growth affects neither.  More precisely,
$n$ enters the household's problem twice and the two effects cancel exactly: it lowers the
effective return on assets from $r$ to $r-n$ (assets must be spread over more people) and it
lowers the effective discount rate from $\rho$ to $\rho-n$ (there are more people to enjoy
consumption), so the difference $r-\rho$ that drives the Euler equation is untouched.  This is the
same cancellation as in Exercise 8.1.

Population growth is not entirely without effect.  From \bk{(11.17)},
\[
s=\frac{A-\rho+\theta n+(\theta-1)\delta}{\theta A} ,
\]
which is strictly increasing in $n$: a faster-growing population requires more capital widening,
so the economy saves more.  And aggregate output grows at $g^{*}+n$ rather than $g^{*}$.  What is
invariant is the growth rate of the \emph{per capita} variables.

\emph{Is it plausible?}  The prediction sits at a knife edge between two forces that the models of
this chapter both switch off.

\emph{Against, from above: scale effects.}  In the Romer model of Section 11.4 the growth rate is
$\theta^{-1}(\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)-\delta-\rho)$, which is strictly increasing in $L$, so a larger
population grows faster --- and if population were growing the growth rate would rise without
bound (Exercise 11.18).  Any model in which ideas are nonrival has this feature: more people means
more people generating ideas that everyone can use.  The very long-run evidence discussed in
Chapter 4 --- accelerating growth over millennia as population grew --- favors some scale effect.
Chapter 13 shows how to keep the idea without the counterfactual acceleration.

\emph{Against, from below: diminishing returns to a fixed factor.}  In a Malthusian economy with
land, faster population growth lowers income per capita.  The $AK$ models have no fixed factor at
all --- indeed Section 11.1 has no factor but capital --- so this channel is absent too.

The models of this chapter therefore predict exact neutrality precisely because they contain
neither of the two mechanisms that would break it.  Empirically the cross-country correlation
between population growth and growth in income per capita is weakly negative rather than zero,
though, as Chapter 4 discusses, the direction of causation is very hard to establish.  Exact
neutrality is best read as a benchmark, not a prediction.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.12}{}
Replace \bk{(11.27)} by a general constant-returns technology in the consumption sector,
$C(t)=G\bigl(K_C(t),L_C(t)\bigr)$, keeping $I(t)=AK_I(t)$.  Write $g(k_C)\equiv G(k_C,1)$ for the
intensive form, with $k_C\equiv K_C/L_C$.

\emph{Along any growth path, $k_C\to\infty$.}  Labor is used only in the consumption sector and is
in fixed supply, so $L_C=L$ is constant.  On a BGP the share $\kappa$ of capital in the investment
sector is constant, so $K_C=(1-\kappa)K$ grows at the rate $g_K>0$ of the capital stock.  Hence
\[
k_C(t)=\frac{(1-\kappa)K(t)}{L}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty .
\]

\emph{The factor shares.}  Labor income arises only in the consumption sector, so with $p_C=1$ the
wage bill is
\[
w(t)L=G\bigl(K_C,L\bigr)-K_CG_K\bigl(K_C,L\bigr)=\bigl[1-\sigma(k_C)\bigr]C(t),
\qquad
\sigma(k)\equiv\frac{k\,g'(k)}{g(k)} ,
\]
$\sigma$ being the elasticity of the consumption technology with respect to capital.  GDP at
current prices is $p_CC+p_II$, so the labor share of GDP is
\[
\frac{w(t)L}{C(t)+p_I(t)I(t)}=\bigl[1-\sigma(k_C(t))\bigr]\cdot
\frac{C(t)}{C(t)+p_I(t)I(t)} .
\]
On a BGP the two components of GDP must grow at the same rate --- otherwise their ratio diverges
and the composition of output is not constant --- so the second factor is constant.  The labor
share of GDP is therefore constant if and only if $\sigma(k_C(t))$ is constant.

\emph{Only Cobb--Douglas has constant $\sigma$.}  Suppose $\sigma(k)=\alpha$ for all $k$ in an
interval.  Then
\[
\frac{\dd\log g(k)}{\dd\log k}=\alpha
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\log g(k)=\alpha\log k+\text{const}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g(k)=Bk^{\alpha} ,
\]
that is $G(K_C,L_C)=BK_C^{\alpha}L_C^{1-\alpha}$.  Since $k_C$ ranges over an unbounded interval
along the BGP, constancy of $\sigma$ over that whole range forces Cobb--Douglas globally.
Conversely, for any non-Cobb--Douglas constant-returns $G$ the share drifts.  For the CES family
$g(k)=B\bigl[\gamma k^{(\sigma_E-1)/\sigma_E}+1-\gamma\bigr]^{\sigma_E/(\sigma_E-1)}$ with
elasticity of substitution $\sigma_E$,
\[
\sigma(k)\longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if }\sigma_E>1,\\
0 & \text{if }\sigma_E<1,
\end{cases}
\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty ,
\]
so the capital share in the consumption sector tends to one or to zero. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why this matters.}  The two-sector model was introduced precisely to deliver sustained growth
\emph{with} constant factor shares, avoiding the baseline $AK$ model's capital share of one.  This
exercise shows the achievement is fragile: it rests on Cobb--Douglas, which is exactly the
functional form that makes factor shares independent of factor proportions.  Since the model's
whole point is that the capital-labor ratio in the consumption sector rises without bound, any
departure from unit elasticity of substitution lets the shares drift --- and if $\sigma_E>1$ the
model reproduces the very defect it was built to avoid.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.13}{}
From \bk{(11.33)} and \bk{(11.34)},
\[
g_K^{*}=\frac{A-\delta-\rho}{1-\alpha(1-\theta)},
\qquad
g_C^{*}=\alpha\,g_K^{*}=\frac{\alpha(A-\delta-\rho)}{1-\alpha(1-\theta)} .
\]
Differentiating, and writing $D\equiv1-\alpha(1-\theta)>0$,
\[
\boxed{\
\frac{\partial g_K^{*}}{\partial\alpha}=\frac{(A-\delta-\rho)(1-\theta)}{D^{2}},
\qquad
\frac{\partial g_C^{*}}{\partial\alpha}=\frac{A-\delta-\rho}{D^{2}}>0 .\ }
\]
So consumption, output and wages always grow faster when $\alpha$ is larger, but the capital stock
does so only when $\theta<1$; for $\theta>1$ capital grows \emph{more slowly}, and for $\theta=1$
its growth rate is independent of $\alpha$ altogether.  With $A=0.15$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$
the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 & \alpha=0.3 & \alpha=0.5 & \alpha=0.7\\\hline
\theta=0.5\quad(g_K^{*},g_C^{*}) & (0.0941,0.0282) & (0.1067,0.0533) & (0.1231,0.0862)\\
\theta=1 & (0.0800,0.0240) & (0.0800,0.0400) & (0.0800,0.0560)\\
\theta=2 & (0.0615,0.0185) & (0.0533,0.0267) & (0.0471,0.0329)\\
\theta=3 & (0.0500,0.0150) & (0.0400,0.0200) & (0.0333,0.0233)
\end{array}
\]

\emph{Why consumption growth rises.}  Two channels, both positive.  The \emph{technological}
channel is direct: $\alpha$ is the elasticity of consumption output with respect to capital, and
$g_C=\alpha g_K$, so a given rate of capital accumulation buys more consumption growth.  The
\emph{price} channel works through the return.  By \bk{(11.30)} the relative price of investment
goods falls at the rate $(1-\alpha)g_K$, so the consumption-denominated return is
\[
r_C=A-\delta-(1-\alpha)g_K .
\]
A higher $\alpha$ means the consumption sector uses capital more intensively, so the same capital
deepening depresses the price of investment goods by less, and $r_C$ is higher.  A higher return
raises consumption growth through the Euler equation.

\emph{Why capital growth can fall.}  The higher return raises $g_C$, but $g_K=g_C/\alpha$ and
$\alpha$ has risen too.  Which effect wins is exactly the usual income-versus-substitution
question: when $\theta>1$ the income effect of a higher return dominates, the household does not
increase saving proportionately, and $g_K$ falls even though $g_C$ rises.  Intuitively, when
capital is more productive in making consumption goods, the economy needs \emph{less} capital
accumulation to deliver any given consumption growth, and a sufficiently income-driven household
takes part of the gain as a lower rate of accumulation.

\rmk{The exercise asks ``Why does it increase the rate of capital accumulation?'' --- which is
correct only for $\theta<1$.  The unambiguous statement is about consumption (and hence output and
wages), whose growth rate rises with $\alpha$ at every $\theta$.  Since most estimates put
$\theta>1$, the empirically relevant case is the one in which $\alpha$ and $g_K^{*}$ move in
\emph{opposite} directions.  The rate of capital deepening, $g_K^{*}-g_C^{*}=(1-\alpha)g_K^{*}$,
falls with $\alpha$ for every $\theta$: a more capital-intensive consumption sector means less
divergence between the capital stock and output, which is the sense in which raising $\alpha$
moves the model back toward the Kaldor facts.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.19}{}
\emph{The wedge.}  By \bk{(11.39)} and \bk{Proposition 11.6},
\[
g_C^{*}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)-\delta-\rho\bigr),
\qquad
g_C^{S}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\wt f(L)-\delta-\rho\bigr),
\]
so the entire gap is the term
\[
L\wt f'(L)=\frac{w(t)L}{K(t)} ,
\]
using $w(t)=K(t)\wt f'(L)$ from \bk{(11.37)}.  Firms pay capital its \emph{private} marginal
product $\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)$, but a unit of capital also raises $A=BK$ and so raises every
firm's output; the external part of its social marginal product is exactly the wage bill per unit
of capital.  Any policy that closes the gap must raise the household's net return from
$\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)-\delta$ to $\wt f(L)-\delta$.

\emph{Policy 1: a Pigouvian subsidy to capital.}  Pay a flow subsidy of $\varsigma$ units of
output per unit of capital, financed by lump-sum taxes, and set
\[
\varsigma^{*}=L\wt f'(L) .
\]
The household's net return becomes $\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)+\varsigma^{*}-\delta=\wt f(L)-\delta$, so
its Euler equation delivers $g_C^{S}$ and the equilibrium coincides with the optimum.  As a
proportional subsidy to capital income the rate is
\[
\zeta^{*}=\frac{L\wt f'(L)}{\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)}
=\frac{\text{labor share}}{\text{capital share}} ,
\]
which \emph{exceeds} one whenever the labor share exceeds the capital share.  With
$F=K^{\alpha}(AL)^{1-\alpha}$, $A=BK$ and $\alpha=1/3$, the program gives $\zeta^{*}=2$: the
subsidy must be $200\%$ of capital income.

The financing has a striking property.  The total cost of the subsidy is $\varsigma^{*}K(t)
=L\wt f'(L)K(t)=w(t)L$ --- exactly the wage bill, at every date.  Since labor is supplied
inelastically, a tax on labor income is lump-sum here, so
\begin{quote}
\emph{a $100\%$ tax on labor income, handed to capital, balances the budget instant by instant and
implements the optimum.}
\end{quote}
Calibrating $\wt f(L)=0.30$ (a capital-output ratio of $3.3$) with $\alpha=1/3$, $\delta=0.05$,
$\rho=0.02$ and $\theta=2$, the program gives $R=0.10$, an equilibrium growth rate of $1.5\%$ and
an optimal growth rate of $11.5\%$.

\emph{Policy 2: a consumption tax falling over time.}  Let consumers face the price
$q(t)$ per unit of consumption, so that the budget constraint is $\dt a=ra+w-q(t)c$.  The
current-value Hamiltonian gives $c^{-\theta}=\mu q$, hence
\[
\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(r-\rho-\frac{\dt q}{q}\Bigr) .
\]
Setting
\[
\frac{\dt q(t)}{q(t)}=-L\wt f'(L)
\]
--- a consumption tax whose rate declines, equivalently a consumption subsidy growing at the rate
$L\wt f'(L)$, financed by lump-sum taxes --- again yields $g_C=g_C^{S}$.  This policy leaves the
return to capital untouched and works purely on the intertemporal price of consumption.

\emph{Why these are genuinely different.}  Policy 1 corrects the externality where it arises, at
the margin at which capital is rewarded; it would remain correct if the household's preferences
changed.  Policy 2 corrects the household's intertemporal margin instead, and its required rate
happens to coincide only because the two margins are linked one for one in this model.  A third
variant --- an investment subsidy at a \emph{constant} rate, lowering the purchase price of capital
goods once and for all --- does \emph{not} work: a constant relative price leaves the rate of
return unchanged, so it produces a level effect and no growth effect at all.

\rmk{The size of the required intervention is the real content here.  The optimal subsidy is not a
small correction but a transfer of the entire wage bill to capital, and it triples the growth rate
in the calibration above.  That is a measure of how much work the externality \bk{(11.36)} is
doing: assuming $A=BK$ makes the social return to capital the whole output-capital ratio rather
than the capital share of it.  It is a reason to read the Romer model of this section as a
tractable illustration rather than a quantitative theory --- and a reason why Part IV models the
returns to knowledge through the profits of innovators, where the wedge is disciplined by the
market structure rather than assumed.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{11.20}{}
\emph{(a) Equilibrium.}  Firms take $A(t)$ as given, so
\[
R(t)=F_K\bigl(K(t),A(t)L\bigr),
\qquad
w(t)=A(t)F_L\bigl(K(t),A(t)L\bigr) ,
\]
where $F_L$ denotes the derivative with respect to the second argument.  Imposing $A(t)=K(t)$ and
writing $\wt f(L)\equiv F(1,L)$, homogeneity of degree one gives $F(K,KL)=K\wt f(L)$, while
$F_K$ is homogeneous of degree zero, so
\[
R(t)=F_K(1,L)=\wt f(L)-L\wt f'(L)\equiv R,
\qquad
w(t)=K(t)\wt f'(L) ,
\]
both exactly as in continuous time.  $R$ is a constant, independent of $K$.  A competitive
equilibrium is a path $[C(t),K(t),R(t),w(t)]_{t=0}^{\infty}$ such that the household maximizes
$\sum_t\beta^{t}\frac{C(t)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}$ subject to
\[
C(t)+K(t+1)=R(t)K(t)+w(t)L
\]
taking $(R,w)$ as given; firms maximize as above; and $A(t)=K(t)$.  (Full depreciation makes $R$
the gross return.)  Market clearing gives the resource constraint
\[
C(t)+K(t+1)=R K(t)+K(t)L\wt f'(L)=\wt f(L)K(t)=Y(t) .
\]

\emph{(b) The BGP.}  The household's Euler equation is
\[
C(t)^{-\theta}=\beta R\,C(t+1)^{-\theta}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \frac{C(t+1)}{C(t)}=\bigl(\beta R\bigr)^{1/\theta}\equiv1+g\ }
\]
a constant, because $R$ does not depend on $K$.  Positive growth requires
\[
\beta R>1 ,
\]
and finite utility (equivalently the transversality condition
$\lim_{t\to\infty}\beta^{t}C(t)^{-\theta}K(t+1)=0$, which reduces to
$\beta(1+g)^{1-\theta}<1$) requires
\[
\beta R^{1-\theta}<1 .
\]
Together,
\[
\frac{1}{R}<\beta<R^{\theta-1} ,
\]
an interval that is nonempty if and only if $R^{\theta}>1$, that is $R>1$: the private gross return
must exceed one, which with full depreciation is the requirement that capital more than replace
itself.  Positive consumption also needs $\wt f(L)>1+g$, which holds automatically for
$\theta\ge1$ since then $(\beta R)^{1/\theta}\le\beta R<R<\wt f(L)$.  With
$F=K^{\alpha}(AL)^{1-\alpha}$, $\alpha=1/3$ and $\wt f(L)=4$, so that $R=\alpha\wt f(L)=4/3$, the
program gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\theta & 0.5 & 1 & 2 & 3\\
\text{admissible }\beta & (0.750,0.866) & (0.750,1.000) & (0.750,1.333) & (0.750,1.778)\\
1+g\ \text{at }\beta=0.8 & 1.1378 & 1.0667 & 1.0328 & 1.0217\\
\text{planner's }1+g & 10.24 & 3.200 & 1.7889 & 1.4736
\end{array}
\]
