unchanged: the price \bk{(14.6)}, the quantity \bk{(14.7)}, profits \bk{(14.8)}, the value
function \bk{(14.13)}, free entry \bk{(14.14)} and the growth rate \bk{(14.23)}.

\emph{What the argument uses, and where it breaks.}  Three things.  First, \emph{price}
competition with a homogeneous product: under Cournot, two identical firms would each earn
positive duopoly profits, and copying would pay whenever $\epsilon$ was small enough.  Second,
$\epsilon>0$ \emph{strictly}: the conclusion is discontinuous at $\epsilon=0$, where copying
becomes weakly optimal and the equilibrium unravels.  Third, that the copier obtains \emph{the
same} technology, not a better one; a copier who could improve on the technology would be an
entrant, and entry is already in the model.

\rmk{The result belongs to the same family as contestable-markets arguments and Exercise 12.3's
observation that trade secrecy can substitute for patents: what protects the innovator is not the
legal monopoly but the fact that imitation destroys the very rents that would pay for it.  It also
shows that the extreme policy conclusions of Section 13.1.6 and of this chapter --- that weaker
intellectual property rights reduce growth --- do not depend on patents \emph{per se}.  What they
depend on is that the rents from an innovation accrue to whoever makes it, and Section 14.4 is
where that presumption is finally relaxed.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.4}{}
\emph{Uniqueness.}  The BGP is determined by three linear relations: \bk{(14.19)}
$r^{*}+z^{*}=\lambda\eta\beta L$, the Euler equation \bk{(14.20)} $r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, and
the aggregation identity \bk{(14.22)} $g^{*}=(\lambda-1)z^{*}$.  Eliminating $r^{*}$ and $z^{*}$,
\[
\theta g^{*}+\rho+\frac{g^{*}}{\lambda-1}=\lambda\eta\beta L
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g^{*}=\frac{\lambda\eta\beta L-\rho}{\theta+(\lambda-1)^{-1}} ,
\]
which is \bk{(14.23)}; the system is linear with a nonzero coefficient on $g^{*}$, so the solution
is unique.

\emph{Positivity.}  $g^{*}>0$ if and only if $\lambda\eta\beta L>\rho$, the first inequality of
\bk{Proposition 14.1}.  Then $z^{*}=g^{*}/(\lambda-1)>0$ as well.

\emph{Transversality.}  By \bk{(14.17)} the value of every leading-edge patent is
$q(\nu,t)/(\lambda\eta)$, so total corporate wealth is
\[
\int_{0}^{1}V(\nu,t\mid q)\dd\nu=\frac{Q(t)}{\lambda\eta} ,
\]
which grows at $g^{*}$.  Condition \bk{(14.16)} therefore holds if and only if $g^{*}<r^{*}$.
Using $r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, this is $(1-\theta)g^{*}<\rho$, and substituting \bk{(14.23)},
\[
(1-\theta)\bigl(\lambda\eta\beta L-\rho\bigr)<\rho\Bigl[\theta+\frac{1}{\lambda-1}\Bigr]
\iff
(1-\theta)\lambda\eta\beta L<\rho\Bigl[1+\frac{1}{\lambda-1}\Bigr]=\rho\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1} ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ (1-\theta)(\lambda-1)\eta\beta L<\rho\ }
\]
exactly the second inequality of \bk{Proposition 14.1}.  The same inequality makes household
utility finite, by the rearrangement used in Exercise 13.4.

\emph{Positive consumption.}  R\&D spending on line $\nu$ is $Z(\nu,t)=z^{*}q(\nu,t)/\eta$, so
$Z(t)=z^{*}Q(t)/\eta$, while net output is
$\wt Y=Y-X=\bigl[\tfrac{1}{1-\beta}-(1-\beta)\bigr]QL=\tfrac{\beta(2-\beta)}{1-\beta}QL$.  Hence
\[
C(t)=Q(t)\Bigl[\frac{\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta}-\frac{z^{*}}{\eta}\Bigr] ,
\]
positive provided $z^{*}/\eta<\beta(2-\beta)L/(1-\beta)$.  This is automatic when $\theta\ge1$:
then $\theta(\lambda-1)+1\ge\lambda$, so
$z^{*}/\eta=\bigl[\lambda\beta L-\rho/\eta\bigr]/[\theta(\lambda-1)+1]\le\beta L$, which is less
than $\beta(2-\beta)L/(1-\beta)$.  With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$
and $\lambda=2$ the program gives $g^{*}=0.0333$, $r^{*}=0.0867$, $z^{*}=0.0333$ and
$C/Q=1.500-0.278=1.222>0$. $\blacksquare$
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.5}{}
\emph{Free entry binds at every date.}  By the argument of Exercise 14.1, if free entry were slack
on some interval it would be slack for every machine line, so there would be no R\&D and no growth
on that interval; but then $r$ would have to satisfy $r\ge\lambda\eta\beta L$, and the Euler
equation would give consumption growth $\theta^{-1}(r-\rho)\ge\theta^{-1}(\lambda\eta\beta L-\rho)>0$
while output was constant --- impossible in the long run given the resource constraint.  Hence
$\eta V(\nu,t\mid q)=\lambda^{-1}q(\nu,t)$ for all $t$.

\emph{The interest rate is constant.}  Since $q(\nu,t)$ is constant between innovations, the
free-entry equality gives $\dt V(\nu,t\mid q)=0$ at every date, and \bk{(14.13)} becomes
\[
r(t)+z(t)=\frac{\pi(\nu,t\mid q)}{V(\nu,t\mid q)}=\lambda\eta\beta L
\qquad\text{for all }t .
\]

\emph{The R\&D rate cannot move.}  Write $A\equiv\beta(2-\beta)L/(1-\beta)$, so that
$C(t)=[A-z(t)/\eta]Q(t)$ by Exercise 14.4, and $\dt Q/Q=(\lambda-1)z(t)$ by \bk{(14.21)}.  Then
\[
\frac{\dt C}{C}=(\lambda-1)z(t)-\frac{\dt z(t)/\eta}{A-z(t)/\eta} ,
\]
while the Euler equation with $r(t)=\lambda\eta\beta L-z(t)$ gives
$\dt C/C=\theta^{-1}\bigl(\lambda\eta\beta L-z(t)-\rho\bigr)$.  Equating and rearranging,
\[
\dt z(t)=-\eta\Bigl[A-\frac{z(t)}{\eta}\Bigr]F\bigl(z(t)\bigr),
\qquad
F(z)\equiv\frac{\lambda\eta\beta L-z-\rho}{\theta}-(\lambda-1)z .
\]
$F$ is strictly decreasing, with the unique root $z^{*}$ of \bk{(14.23)}, and $A-z/\eta>0$ at
$z^{*}$ by Exercise 14.4.  Hence
\[
\frac{\partial\dt z}{\partial z}\Big|_{z^{*}}=-\eta\Bigl[A-\frac{z^{*}}{\eta}\Bigr]F'(z^{*})>0 :
\]
the rest point is \emph{unstable}.  Since $z$ is a jump variable --- nothing about the R\&D rate
is predetermined --- any $z(0)\ne z^{*}$ would send $z$ either to zero (contradicting free entry
binding) or above $\eta A$ (making consumption negative).  Therefore $z(t)=z^{*}$ for all $t$,
$r(t)=r^{*}$, and the economy grows at $g^{*}$ from the first instant, whatever $Q(0)>0$.
$\blacksquare$

The economics is the same as in Chapter 13: free entry pins the value of a patent to a constant
multiple of its quality at every date, which pins the effective discount rate $r+z$, which leaves
no room for the interest rate or the R\&D rate to move.  Linearity of the innovation possibilities
frontier is what removes the transitional dynamics, exactly as in the $AK$ model.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.8}{}
\emph{The static step.}  Without markups the planner equates the marginal product of a machine to
its marginal cost: from \bk{(14.3)}, $q x^{-\beta}L^{\beta}=\psi q$, so
$x^{S}=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}L=\chi L$, which is \bk{(14.24)}.  Then
$Y^{S}=\chi Q^{S}L$ and
$X^{S}=\psi\chi Q^{S}L=(1-\beta)\chi Q^{S}L$, so net output is
\[
\wt Y^{S}=\chi Q^{S}L\bigl[1-(1-\beta)\bigr]=\beta\chi\,Q^{S}(t)L ,
\]
which is \bk{(14.25)}.

\emph{(a) Sufficiency.}  The reduced problem has the current-value Hamiltonian
\[
\wh H\bigl(Q^{S},C^{S},\mu^{S}\bigr)=\frac{(C^{S})^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}
+\mu^{S}\eta(\lambda-1)\bigl[\beta\chi Q^{S}L-C^{S}\bigr] ,
\]
strictly concave in $C^{S}$ and linear in $Q^{S}$, hence jointly concave; and
$\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu^{S}(t)Q^{S}(t)\ge0$ for every feasible path, since $\mu^{S}>0$
and $Q^{S}\ge0$.  Provided the objective is finite --- the condition
$(1-\theta)\eta(\lambda-1)\beta\chi L<\rho$ of \bk{Proposition 14.3} --- \bk{Theorem 7.14}
applies, so the listed conditions are sufficient.

\emph{(b) Uniqueness.}  Strict concavity in $C^{S}$ makes the maximizer of the Hamiltonian unique
at each date; the Euler equation then determines the whole path from $C^{S}(0)$, and, exactly as
in Exercise 8.11, the transversality condition selects a single $C^{S}(0)$: a larger one violates
feasibility (capital is driven negative in finite time), a smaller one leaves
$\lim e^{-\rho t}\mu^{S}Q^{S}>0$.

\emph{(c) The growth rate.}  The conditions are
\[
(C^{S})^{-\theta}=\mu^{S}\eta(\lambda-1),
\qquad
\mu^{S}\eta(\lambda-1)\beta\chi L=\rho\mu^{S}-\dt\mu^{S} .
\]
The second gives $\dt\mu^{S}/\mu^{S}=\rho-\eta(\lambda-1)\beta\chi L$, and differentiating the
first gives $\dt\mu^{S}/\mu^{S}=-\theta\dt C^{S}/C^{S}$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ g^{S}=\frac{\dt C^{S}}{C^{S}}
=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\eta(\lambda-1)(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}\beta L-\rho\Bigr)\ }
\]
which is \bk{(14.27)}. $\blacksquare$

As in Exercise 13.10, the right side contains no state variable, so the optimal allocation grows
at $g^{S}$ from $t=0$ with no transitional dynamics; the transversality condition eliminates the
explosive homogeneous solution for $Q^{S}$ and pins $C^{S}(0)$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.9}{}
\emph{What the buyer compares.}  A final good producer facing a machine of quality $q$ at price
$p$ chooses $x=(q/p)^{1/\beta}L$ by \bk{(14.4)}, and its net gain from that machine line is
\[
\frac{1}{1-\beta}qx^{1-\beta}L^{\beta}-px
=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}L\,q^{1/\beta}p^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}} .
\]
So the producer ranks offers by $q^{1/\beta}p^{-(1-\beta)/\beta}$, equivalently by
$q\,p^{-(1-\beta)}$: a machine is better the higher its quality and the lower its price, with
quality weighted more heavily the larger is $\beta$.

\emph{The comparison.}  The innovator offers quality $q$ at the unconstrained monopoly price
$p=\psi q/(1-\beta)$; the previous vintage offers quality $\lambda^{-1}q$ and, under Bertrand
competition, will go down to its marginal cost $\psi\lambda^{-1}q$.  The buyer prefers the new
machine if and only if
\[
\frac{q}{\bigl[\psi q/(1-\beta)\bigr]^{1-\beta}}\ \ge\ \frac{\lambda^{-1}q}{\bigl[\psi\lambda^{-1}q\bigr]^{1-\beta}} .
\]
Cancelling $q^{\beta}\psi^{-(1-\beta)}$ from both sides leaves
\[
(1-\beta)^{1-\beta}\ \ge\ \lambda^{-\beta}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\lambda^{\beta}\ \ge\ (1-\beta)^{-(1-\beta)}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \lambda\ \ge\ \Bigl(\frac{1}{1-\beta}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}}\ }
\]
which is exactly \bk{(14.5)}. $\blacksquare$

With $\beta=1/2$ the threshold is $\lambda^{*}=2$: the quality step must at least double
productivity before the innovator can ignore the machine it replaced.  For $\beta$ near $1$ the
threshold falls to $1$ (quality matters overwhelmingly), and for $\beta$ near $0$ it rises to $e$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.10}{}
\emph{(a) The limit price.}  When \bk{(14.5)} fails the buyer would prefer the old vintage at the
new monopoly price, so the innovator must price low enough to make the buyer indifferent.  Setting
the two ranking indices of Exercise 14.9 equal,
\[
\frac{q}{p^{1-\beta}}=\frac{\lambda^{-1}q}{\bigl(\psi\lambda^{-1}q\bigr)^{1-\beta}}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
p^{1-\beta}=\lambda\bigl(\psi\lambda^{-1}q\bigr)^{1-\beta}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ p_x=\lambda^{\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}}\psi q\ }
\]
This is below the unconstrained monopoly price $\psi q/(1-\beta)$ precisely when
$\lambda^{\beta/(1-\beta)}<(1-\beta)^{-1}$, that is when \bk{(14.5)} fails --- and the two
coincide exactly at $\lambda=\lambda^{*}$, as they must.

\emph{(b) The growth rate.}  Write $\Lambda\equiv\lambda^{\beta/(1-\beta)}$ and keep
$\psi=1-\beta$.  Then $x=(q/p_x)^{1/\beta}L=(\Lambda\psi)^{-1/\beta}L$ and flow profits per unit
of quality are
\[
\wt\pi\equiv\frac{\pi}{q}=(\Lambda-1)\,\Lambda^{-1/\beta}(1-\beta)^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}}L .
\]
Everything else in Exercise 14.1 goes through with $\beta L$ replaced by $\wt\pi$, so
$r^{*}+z^{*}=\lambda\eta\wt\pi$ and
\[
g^{*}=\frac{\lambda\eta\wt\pi-\rho}{\theta+(\lambda-1)^{-1}} .
\]
Setting $\Lambda=(1-\beta)^{-1}$ recovers $\wt\pi=\beta L$ and \bk{(14.23)}.

\emph{(c) The optimum.}  The planner never faces a price, so \bk{(14.27)} is unchanged; the
equilibrium falls further short of it, since $\wt\pi<\beta L$.

\emph{(d) Removing the rival.}  If the previous producer disappears, the innovator charges its
ideal markup and earns $\beta L$ per unit of quality.  Since $p^{M}$ maximizes
$(p-\psi q)x(p)$ and the limit price differs from it, $\beta L>\wt\pi$ strictly, hence
\[
g^{\text{hyp}}=\frac{\lambda\eta\beta L-\rho}{\theta+(\lambda-1)^{-1}}\ >\ g^{*} .
\]
With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$ the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
\lambda & 1.2 & 1.5 & 2.0\ (=\lambda^{*})\\
\text{regime} & \text{limit} & \text{limit} & \text{monopoly}\\
g^{*} & 0.00286 & 0.01500 & 0.03333\\
g^{\text{hyp}} & 0.00743 & 0.01750 & 0.03333
\end{array}
\]

\emph{Explanation.}  Competition from the vintage the innovator replaced forces a lower price,
which lowers flow profits, which lowers the value of a patent, which --- through free entry ---
lowers R\&D and growth.  The effect vanishes exactly when the innovation is large enough to make
the old vintage irrelevant.  This is the same message as Section 13.1.6 and Exercise 13.14:
in this class of models competition is good for static efficiency and bad for growth.  It is worth
noting what makes the message so stark: the old vintage exerts competitive pressure but
contributes nothing to innovation.  Section 14.4, where the laggard is a live R\&D competitor, is
where the sign can flip.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.11}{}
Follow the hint: one unit of the final good spent on R\&D for a machine of quality $q$ generates
a flow rate $\eta/q^{\phi}$ of innovation with $\phi>1$, and let population grow at $n$.

\emph{Free entry.}  Research aimed at a line whose current quality is $\lambda^{-1}q$ generates
quality $q$ at flow rate $\eta/(\lambda^{-1}q)^{\phi}$, so
\[
\frac{\eta}{(\lambda^{-1}q)^{\phi}}\,V(\nu,t\mid q)\le1 ,
\qquad\text{with equality when there is research.}
\]
Flow profits are $\pi=\beta qL(t)$ and grow at $n$ along a BGP, so, as in Exercise 13.18,
\[
V(\nu,t\mid q)=\frac{\beta qL(t)}{r^{*}+z^{*}-n} ,
\]
and free entry becomes
\[
\frac{\eta\lambda^{\phi}\beta L(t)}{r^{*}+z^{*}-n}=q^{\phi-1} .
\]

\emph{The growth rate.}  The left side grows at $n$; the right side grows at $(\phi-1)g_Q$, where
$g_Q$ is the growth rate of quality.  For free entry to hold at every date,
\[
\boxed{\ g^{*}=g_Q=\frac{n}{\phi-1}\ }
\]
Output is $Y=QL/(1-\beta)$, so output per capita $Q/(1-\beta)$ grows at $n/(\phi-1)>0$ while
aggregate output grows at $n/(\phi-1)+n$.  The interest rate follows from the Euler equation,
$r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, and the innovation rate from \bk{(14.22)},
$z^{*}=g^{*}/(\lambda-1)=n/[(\lambda-1)(\phi-1)]$; the level of $Q$ is then pinned down by the
free-entry condition.  A finite $V$ requires $r^{*}+z^{*}>n$.

\emph{What has changed.}  The growth rate depends on $n$ and $\phi$ alone --- not on $L$, $\eta$,
$\beta$ or $\lambda$:
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\phi & 1.25 & 1.5 & 2 & 3\\
g^{*}\ (n=0.01) & 0.040 & 0.020 & 0.010 & 0.005
\end{array}
\]
The scale effect has been removed exactly as in Section 13.3, and for the same reason: research on
a machine of quality $q$ is now \emph{more} than proportionately harder ($\phi>1$ rather than
$\phi=1$), so a growing market is needed to keep the incentive to innovate constant.  As in
Exercise 13.20 a level scale effect survives --- a larger $L$ supports a higher $Q$ at every date
--- and the model becomes semi-endogenous: policy no longer affects the growth rate.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.16}{}
There is a single machine line.  Between innovations its quality $q$ is constant and the
allocation of labor is constant at $(L_E^{*},L_R^{*})$, so output
\[
Y(t)=\frac{q(t)L_E^{*}}{1-\beta}
\]
is constant: the economy does not grow at all.  Output jumps by the factor $\lambda$ at the
instant of each innovation.

Each of the $L_R^{*}$ research workers generates innovations at flow rate $\eta$, independently,
so the aggregate arrival process is Poisson with rate $\eta L_R^{*}$.  The waiting time between
successive arrivals is therefore exponentially distributed with that rate,
\[
\Prob\bigl[\text{no innovation in }[t,t+s]\bigr]=e^{-\eta L_R^{*}s} ,
\]
whose mean is
\[
\boxed{\ \E[\text{interval}]=\frac{1}{\eta L_R^{*}}\ }
\]
$\blacksquare$  With the program's parameters ($\lambda=2$, $\eta=0.15$, $\beta=1/2$, $L=1$,
$\rho=0.05$) one gets $L_R^{*}=1/3$ and an average interval of $20$ periods.

\rmk{This is the price of having one sector rather than a continuum.  In Section 14.1 a law of
large numbers across a continuum of independent machine lines makes $Q(t)$ deterministic and
growth perfectly smooth; here there is nothing to average over.  As the text observes, real
economies lie between the two --- they do not grow at constant rates, but neither do they stand
still for decades and then jump.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.17}{}
\emph{(a) The equilibrium.}  Households are risk neutral, so $r(t)=\rho$ at all dates.  With the
unconstrained monopoly price, $\pi(q)=\beta qL_E$ and $w(q)=\beta q/(1-\beta)$.  A leader is
replaced at the flow rate $\eta L_R$, so
\[
V(q)=\frac{\beta qL_E}{\rho+\eta L_R} .
\]
A research worker generates flow rate $\eta$ of an innovation worth $V(\lambda q)$ and costs
$w(q)$, so free entry requires $\eta V(\lambda q)=w(q)$:
\[
\frac{\eta\lambda\beta qL_E}{\rho+\eta L_R}=\frac{\beta q}{1-\beta}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\eta\lambda(1-\beta)\bigl(L-L_R\bigr)=\rho+\eta L_R ,
\]
whose unique solution is
\[
\boxed{\ L_R^{*}=\frac{\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L-\rho}{\eta\bigl[1+\lambda(1-\beta)\bigr]}\ }
\]
Positive research requires $\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L>\rho$; and $L_R^{*}<L$ holds automatically.

\emph{The growth rate.}  Let $n(t)$ count innovations by date $t$, a Poisson process with rate
$\eta L_R^{*}$.  Then $q(t)=\lambda^{n(t)}q(0)$ and $Y(t)=q(t)L_E^{*}/(1-\beta)$, so
\[
\frac{\log Y(t)-\log Y(0)}{t}=\frac{n(t)}{t}\log\lambda
\ \xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}\ \eta L_R^{*}\log\lambda
\]
almost surely, by the strong law of large numbers for the Poisson process.  Hence
$g^{*}=\eta L_R^{*}\log\lambda$.

\emph{The parameter condition.}  With risk neutrality, utility is $\int_0^\infty e^{-\rho t}C(t)\dd t$
with $C$ growing at $g^{*}$ on average, finite if and only if $\rho>g^{*}$.  Substituting
$L_R^{*}$,
\[
\rho>\log\lambda\,\frac{\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L-\rho}{1+\lambda(1-\beta)}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L<\rho\Bigl[1+\frac{1+\lambda(1-\beta)}{\log\lambda}\Bigr] .
\]
Together with $\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L>\rho$ this is \bk{(14.30)}, and it is necessary and
sufficient for the equilibrium of the proposition. $\blacksquare$

With $\lambda=2$, $\eta=0.15$, $\beta=1/2$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.05$: $L_R^{*}=0.333$,
$g^{*}=0.0347$, and the upper bound is $0.194$ against $\lambda(1-\beta)\eta L=0.15$.

\emph{(b) $\log\lambda$ versus $\lambda-1$.}  The two models average in different places.

In Section 14.1 there is a continuum of machine lines.  In an interval $\dd t$ a fraction
$z\dd t$ of them innovate, each multiplying its quality by $\lambda$, so the \emph{arithmetic
average} $Q=\int_0^1q\dd\nu$ grows deterministically at
\[
\frac{\dt Q}{Q}=(\lambda-1)z ,
\]
and since output is linear in $Q$, that is the growth rate of output.

In Section 14.2 there is one line.  Its quality is $\lambda^{n(t)}q(0)$, a pure jump process, and
what obeys a law of large numbers is not $q$ but $\log q$:
\[
\frac{\log q(t)}{t}\to z\log\lambda .
\]
The long-run growth rate of the \emph{level} of output is governed by the geometric mean, not the
arithmetic one, and Jensen's inequality gives $\log\lambda<\lambda-1$.  (Indeed $\E[q(t)]$ does
grow at $(\lambda-1)z$ in the one-sector model too, but that expectation is driven by rare paths
with unusually many innovations and is not the rate almost every realization exhibits.)  With
$\lambda=2$ the difference is $0.693$ against $1$ --- a third of the growth rate.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.23}{}
\emph{(a) The BGP.}  Since profits $\beta qL$ and both R\&D costs are proportional to $q$, the
value function is linear, $V(q)=vq$.  The incumbent's first-order condition \bk{(14.43)} then
gives $\phi(\lambda-1)v=1$, so
\[
v=\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda-1)} ,
\]
which is \bk{(14.44)}.  Free entry \bk{(14.41)} gives $\eta(\wh z)\kappa v=1$, hence
\[
\eta(\wh z^{*})=\frac{\phi(\lambda-1)}{\kappa}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\wh z^{*}=\eta^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{\phi(\lambda-1)}{\kappa}\Bigr) ,
\]
which is \bk{(14.45)}: unique because $\eta$ is continuous and strictly decreasing, and strictly
positive by the Inada conditions \bk{(14.34)}.  Equating $vq$ with \bk{(14.46)},
\[
r^{*}=\frac{\beta L}{v}-\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})=\phi(\lambda-1)\beta L-\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*}) ,
\]
which is \bk{(14.47)}, and the Euler equation gives \bk{(14.48)}.  Every step is an equality, so
the BGP growth rate is unique.

\emph{Incumbent R\&D is positive.}  From the decomposition \bk{(14.52)},
\[
z^{*}=\frac{g^{*}-(\kappa-1)\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})}{(\lambda-1)\phi}\ >\ 0
\iff
\phi(\lambda-1)\beta L-\bigl[1+\theta(\kappa-1)\bigr]\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})>\rho ,
\]
using \bk{(14.48)} --- the first inequality of \bk{(14.53)}.

\emph{Transversality.}  Total corporate wealth is $\int_0^1V(\nu,t\mid q)\dd\nu=vQ(t)$, growing at
$g^{*}$, so \bk{(14.16)} holds iff $g^{*}<r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, that is $(1-\theta)g^{*}<\rho$,
which rearranges to
\[
\rho>(1-\theta)\Bigl(\phi(\lambda-1)\beta L-\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})\Bigr) ,
\]
the second inequality of \bk{(14.53)}.

\emph{(b) Uniqueness of the dynamic path.}  Suppose first that \bk{(14.43)} holds everywhere.
Linearity gives $V(\nu,t\mid q)=v(t)q$, and \bk{(14.43)} reads $\phi(\lambda-1)v(t)=1$ for all
$t$.  Differentiating, $\dt v(t)=0$, so $v(t)=v$ is constant.  Free entry
$\eta(\wh z(t))\kappa v=1$ then forces $\wh z(t)=\wh z^{*}$ at every date, and \bk{(14.40)}
becomes
\[
r(t)v=\beta L-\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})v ,
\]
so $r(t)=r^{*}$ for all $t$.  The Euler equation then gives $g(t)=g^{*}$: every variable takes its
BGP value immediately.

If instead \bk{(14.43)} fails on some set of lines --- so that those incumbents do no incremental
R\&D --- then \bk{(14.40)} with $z=0$ gives a differential equation for $\wh z(\nu,t\mid q)$
along those lines.  Its unique rest point is the BGP allocation, and, exactly as in Exercise 14.5,
the rest point is \emph{unstable}: a higher $\wh z$ lowers the value of incumbency, which lowers
$V(\kappa q)$, which by free entry calls forth still more entry.  Since $\wh z$ is a jump
variable, it must sit at the rest point.  Hence there is no equilibrium other than the BGP.
$\blacksquare$
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.24}{}
Free entry by entrants holds with equality whenever there is entry, \bk{(14.41)}:
\[
\eta\bigl(\wh z\bigr)V(\nu,t\mid\kappa q)=q ,
\]
that is, one unit of final good spent on radical R\&D buys a flow rate $\eta(\wh z)/q$ of an
innovation worth $V(\kappa q)$, and the two exactly offset: entrants earn zero.

Now suppose the incumbent --- which currently owns a machine of quality $q$ worth $V(q)>0$ ---
spends one unit on the same technology.  With probability flow $\eta(\wh z)/q$ it obtains the new
machine of quality $\kappa q$ worth $V(\kappa q)$, \emph{but in doing so it destroys its own
existing machine}, giving up $V(q)$.  Its net return per unit spent is therefore
\[
\frac{\eta(\wh z)}{q}\Bigl[V(\kappa q)-V(q)\Bigr]-1
\ <\ \frac{\eta(\wh z)}{q}V(\kappa q)-1=0 ,
\]
strictly negative because $V(q)>0$.  The incumbent therefore sets its radical R\&D to zero.
$\blacksquare$

This is Arrow's replacement effect of \bk{Proposition 12.3} in general equilibrium: the incumbent
values the same innovation strictly less than an entrant does, by exactly the profits it would
replace, and free entry has already driven the entrant's return to zero.  It also explains the
structure of Section 14.3: incumbents innovate only with the \emph{incremental} technology, which
entrants cannot use and which improves rather than replaces the incumbent's own machine, so that
the loss $V(q)$ is not incurred --- the incumbent moves from $V(q)$ to $V(\lambda q)$ instead.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.25}{}
\emph{(a) The problem.}  Pricing machines at marginal cost gives $x^{S}=\chi L$ and net output
$\chi\beta QL$, as in Exercise 14.8.  R\&D absorbs $(z+\wh z)Q$ by \bk{(14.35)}, and average
quality evolves by \bk{(14.51)}.  So the planner solves
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\frac{C^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
C=\bigl[\chi\beta L-z-\wh z\bigr]Q,
\qquad
\frac{\dt Q}{Q}=(\lambda-1)\phi z+(\kappa-1)f(\wh z) ,
\]
writing $f(\wh z)\equiv\wh z\,\eta(\wh z)$, which is increasing by assumption; assume it is also
concave, as the congestion interpretation suggests.  The current-value Hamiltonian
\[
\wh H=\frac{\bigl([\chi\beta L-z-\wh z]Q\bigr)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}
+\mu Q\bigl[(\lambda-1)\phi z+(\kappa-1)f(\wh z)\bigr]
\]
is then concave in the controls (a concave increasing transform of a linear function, plus a
concave term) and \bk{Theorem 7.14} applies.

\emph{The first-order conditions.}  Differentiating in $z$ and in $\wh z$,
\[
C^{-\theta}=\mu(\lambda-1)\phi ,
\qquad
C^{-\theta}=\mu(\kappa-1)f'(\wh z) ,
\]
so the planner's entry rate solves
\[
\boxed{\ (\lambda-1)\phi=(\kappa-1)f'\bigl(\wh z^{S}\bigr)\ }
\]
against the market's $(\lambda-1)\phi=\kappa\,\eta(\wh z^{*})=\kappa f(\wh z^{*})/\wh z^{*}$.
Since $f$ is concave with $f(0)=0$, $f'(\wh z)<f(\wh z)/\wh z$, and $\kappa>\kappa-1$, so
\[
(\kappa-1)f'(\wh z)<\kappa\frac{f(\wh z)}{\wh z}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\wh z^{S}<\wh z^{*} :
\]
\emph{the planner always chooses less entry than the market}, for two reasons at once --- entrants
ignore the congestion they impose on other entrants ($f'<f/\wh z$) and they count the whole step
$\kappa$ rather than the increment $\kappa-1$ (business stealing).

Differentiating in $Q$ and using $C^{-\theta}=\mu(\lambda-1)\phi$, the costate equation and the
requirement that $C/Q$ be constant give
\[
\boxed{\ g^{S}=\frac{(\lambda-1)\phi\bigl[\chi\beta L-\wh z^{S}\bigr]+(\kappa-1)f(\wh z^{S})-\rho}{\theta}\ }
\]

\emph{(b) The comparison.}  Subtracting \bk{(14.48)},
\[
\theta\bigl(g^{S}-g^{*}\bigr)
=\underbrace{(\lambda-1)\phi\beta L\bigl(\chi-1\bigr)}_{\text{no markup}}
+\underbrace{(\kappa-1)\Bigl[f(\wh z^{S})-\wh z^{S}f'(\wh z^{S})\Bigr]}_{\text{less entry}}
+\underbrace{f(\wh z^{*})}_{\text{entry lowers }g^{*}} .
\]
Every term is nonnegative: the first because $\chi>1$; the second because $f$ is concave with
$f(0)=0$; the third trivially.  Hence
\[
g^{S}>g^{*}\qquad\text{always.}
\]
With $\eta(z)=\eta_0z^{-\gamma}$ the program confirms this at every parameter combination tried:
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
(\phi,\lambda,\kappa,\eta_0,\gamma) & \wh z^{*} & \wh z^{S} & g^{*} & g^{S}\\
(0.6,1.2,2.0,0.0219,0.5) & 0.1332 & 0.0083 & 0.0160 & 0.1105\\
(0.6,1.2,3.0,0.0150,0.5) & 0.1406 & 0.0156 & 0.0172 & 0.1109\\
(0.9,1.3,2.0,0.0300,0.5) & 0.0494 & 0.0031 & 0.0542 & 0.2604
\end{array}
\]

\rmk{The exercise asks for numerical examples in which the optimal growth rate is greater
\emph{or less} than the equilibrium one.  The displayed identity shows the second cannot happen
once $f=\wh z\eta(\wh z)$ is concave --- which is needed anyway for the planner's problem to be
well behaved.  The intuition the exercise offers is nonetheless right as far as it goes: the
planner does cut entry, and cutting entry does remove one source of growth.  But in this model
more entry \emph{lowers} the equilibrium growth rate as well, through \bk{(14.48)}, because it
shortens the incumbent's expected life and so discourages incremental innovation.  Cutting entry
therefore pushes both growth rates the same way, and the markup effect --- worth a factor
$\chi\ge e$ --- settles the comparison.  A genuine example of excessive growth needs the incumbent
R\&D margin to be absent, as in Section 14.1 with non-drastic innovations, or a convex $f$.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.28}{}
\emph{The equilibrium is unchanged.}  With $Y=\frac{1}{1-\beta}\bigl[\int_0^1qx^{1-\beta}\dd\nu\bigr]L^{\beta}$
and marginal cost $\psi q$, Exercise 14.1's computation applies verbatim: demand is
$x=(q/p_x)^{1/\beta}L$, the monopoly price is $p_x=q$ after the normalization $\psi=1-\beta$,
and
\[
x(\nu,t\mid q)=L,
\qquad
\pi(\nu,t\mid q)=\beta q(\nu,t)L .
\]
Profits are exactly as in \bk{(14.8)}, so the value functions \bk{(14.40)}, the free-entry
condition \bk{(14.41)}, the incumbent's condition \bk{(14.42)}, the entry rate \bk{(14.45)}, the
growth rate \bk{(14.48)} and the decomposition \bk{(14.52)} are all unchanged.

\emph{But firm size is constant.}  Sales in units are $x=L$ for \emph{every} firm, regardless of
its quality.  The stochastic process \bk{(14.54)} collapses: a firm's size never changes while it
survives, and drops to zero when it is replaced.  There are no firm-size dynamics.

\emph{Why.}  In Section 14.3 the marginal cost of a machine is $\psi$, independent of quality, so
a better machine is cheaper per unit of quality and final good producers buy more of it:
$x=qL$.  Here the cost is $\psi q$, proportional to quality, so improving a machine raises its
price and its cost in the same proportion and leaves the quantity untouched.  Quality shows up
entirely in the price, not in the quantity.

\emph{Profits do have dynamics.}  Flow profits $\pi=\beta qL$ are proportional to quality, so for
small $\Delta t$
\[
\pi(\nu,t+\Delta t)=
\begin{cases}
\lambda\,\pi(\nu,t) & \text{with probability }\phi z^{*}\Delta t+o(\Delta t),\\
0 & \text{with probability }\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})\Delta t+o(\Delta t),\\
\pi(\nu,t) & \text{otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\]
exactly the process \bk{(14.54)} had for sales.

\emph{The stationary distribution.}  A firm that has survived to age $a$ has taken a
Poisson$(\phi z^{*}a)$ number of incremental steps, and age is exponential with rate
$\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})$.  Integrating,
\[
\Prob[n=k]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\wh z^{*}\eta\,e^{-\wh z^{*}\eta a}\,
\frac{(\phi z^{*}a)^{k}}{k!}e^{-\phi z^{*}a}\dd a
=(1-p)\,p^{k},
\qquad
p\equiv\frac{\phi z^{*}}{\phi z^{*}+\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})} ,
\]
a geometric distribution.  Relative profits are $\lambda^{k}$, so
\[
\Prob\bigl[\pi/\pi_{0}\ge\lambda^{k}\bigr]=p^{k}=\bigl(\lambda^{k}\bigr)^{-\zeta},
\qquad
\boxed{\ \zeta=\frac{\log(1/p)}{\log\lambda}\ }
\]
--- a \emph{Pareto} tail with index $\zeta$.  With the program's parameters
$\phi z^{*}=0.0400$ and $\wh z^{*}\eta(\wh z^{*})=0.0080$, giving $p=0.834$ and $\zeta=1.00$:
Zipf's law, which is what the firm-size data show.

\rmk{This is worth separating from the exercise's main point.  Random proportional growth
truncated by an exponential exit hazard generates a Pareto distribution whatever the variable
being grown --- sales in Section 14.3, profits here.  The reason the baseline model of Section
14.1 is usually described as having no firm dynamics is that its natural size measure, physical
sales, is constant; measured by revenue $p_xx=qL$ or by profits, the same power law is there.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.29}{}
Firm $i$ leads with marginal cost $w/q_i$, firm $-i$ follows with $w/q_{-i}\ge w/q_i$, the two
varieties are perfect substitutes, and they compete in prices.

\emph{The unconstrained monopoly price is infinite.}  Demand \bk{(14.60)} is $y=Y/p^{y}$, which is
\emph{unit elastic}: revenue $p^{y}y=Y$ does not depend on the price at all.  Profits are
therefore
\[
\bigl(p^{y}-MC\bigr)\frac{Y}{p^{y}}=Y\Bigl(1-\frac{MC}{p^{y}}\Bigr) ,
\]
strictly increasing in $p^{y}$ without bound.  An unconstrained monopolist would charge an
arbitrarily high price; what pins the price down is entirely the rival.

\emph{Bertrand.}  If the leader charged $p>w/q_{-i}$, the follower could set a price in
$(w/q_{-i},p)$, capture the whole market and make a strictly positive margin --- so no such $p$
is an equilibrium.  If the leader charged $p<w/q_{-i}$ it would still capture the whole market
(the follower cannot profitably match) but would earn less than at $w/q_{-i}$, by the
monotonicity just established.  Hence the unique equilibrium price is
\[
\boxed{\ p^{y}_{i}(\nu,t)=\frac{w(t)}{q_{-i}(\nu,t)}\ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

Substituting into \bk{(14.60)} gives \bk{(14.64)}, $y_i=q_{-i}Y/w$, and the profits \bk{(14.69)},
\[
\Pi_i=\Bigl(\frac{w}{q_{-i}}-\frac{w}{q_i}\Bigr)\frac{q_{-i}Y}{w}
=\Bigl(1-\frac{q_{-i}}{q_i}\Bigr)Y=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-n}\bigr)Y ,
\]
which depend only on the technology gap $n$ and on aggregate output.  That is the feature which
makes the gap the single industry-level state variable, and it is a consequence of the
Cobb--Douglas aggregator \bk{(14.59)}: with any other elasticity the profits would depend on the
levels of $q_i$ and $q_{-i}$ separately, and the state space would be much larger.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.30}{}
Write $q(\nu,t)\equiv q_i(\nu,t)$ for the leader's quality, so that the follower's is
$q_{-i}=q(\nu,t)\lambda^{-n(\nu,t)}$.  Take logarithms of \bk{(14.59)} and substitute the
equilibrium quantity \bk{(14.64)}, which is $y(\nu,t)=q_{-i}(\nu,t)Y(t)/w(t)$:
\[
\log Y(t)=\int_{0}^{1}\log y(\nu,t)\dd\nu
=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl[\log q(\nu,t)-n(\nu,t)\log\lambda+\log\frac{Y(t)}{w(t)}\Bigr]\dd\nu .
\]
The last term does not depend on $\nu$, so it integrates to itself; the first is $\log Q(t)$ by
the definition \bk{(14.74)}; and the second integrates to $\log\lambda$ times the mean technology
gap,
\[
\int_{0}^{1}n(\nu,t)\dd\nu=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\,\mu_n(t) .
\]
Hence
\[
\log Y(t)=\log Q(t)-\log\lambda\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\mu_n(t)+\log Y(t)-\log w(t) ,
\]
and cancelling $\log Y(t)$ from both sides leaves
\[
\boxed{\ w(t)=Q(t)\,\lambda^{-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\mu_n(t)}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(14.75)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Reading the formula.}  The wage is the aggregate quality index discounted by the average
technology gap.  Two economies with the same $Q$ but different distributions of gaps pay different
wages: the one with larger gaps has higher markups, since by \bk{(14.69)} a gap of $n$ supports a
markup factor $\lambda^{n}$, and so a smaller labor share.  The mean gap $\sum n\mu_n$ is thus a
sufficient statistic for the aggregate markup in this economy, which is why competition policy
here works through the \emph{composition} of industries and not only through their R\&D
decisions.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.31}{}
By \bk{(14.81)},
\[
z_n^{*}=\max\Bigl\{G'^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{\delta_{n+1}}{\omega^{*}}\Bigr),\,0\Bigr\},
\qquad \delta_{n+1}\equiv v_{n+1}-v_n .
\]
The first part of \bk{Proposition 14.8} establishes that $\{v_n\}_{n=0}^{\infty}$ is bounded and
strictly increasing, hence convergent, so its increments vanish:
\[
\delta_{n+1}\longrightarrow0\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty .
\]
Now use the assumption $G'(0)>0$ --- the absence of an Inada condition at zero R\&D.  Since $G'$
is strictly increasing with $G'(0)>0$, the inverse $G'^{-1}(y)$ is defined and positive only for
$y>G'(0)$; for $y\le G'(0)$ the maximum in \bk{(14.81)} is attained at $z=0$.  Therefore
\[
\delta_{n+1}\le\omega^{*}G'(0)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad z_n^{*}=0 .
\]
Because $\omega^{*}>0$ and $\delta_{n+1}\to0$, there exists $n^{*}\ge1$ such that
$\delta_{n+1}\le\omega^{*}G'(0)$ for all $n\ge n^{*}$, and hence
\[
\boxed{\ z_n^{*}=0\qquad\text{for all }n\ge n^{*} .\ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What drives it.}  Two ingredients.  Flow profits $1-\lambda^{-n}$ are bounded above by one,
so the gain from moving one more step ahead shrinks geometrically: a leader $n$ steps ahead
already captures almost the whole available markup, and step $n+1$ adds only
$\lambda^{-n}-\lambda^{-n-1}$.  And $G'(0)>0$ means R\&D has a strictly positive marginal cost
even at zero, so an arbitrarily small benefit does not justify any effort at all.  Without that
assumption --- with an Inada condition at zero --- every leader would do some R\&D, however far
ahead, and there would be no $n^{*}$.

This is what makes the composition effects of Section 14.4 operative: industries that drift too far
from neck-and-neck stop contributing to growth altogether, so policies that pull industries back
toward $n=0$ can raise aggregate R\&D even though they reduce the reward to leadership.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.32}{}
Since $G'^{-1}$ is strictly increasing, $z_0^{*}>z_1^{*}$ is equivalent to
$\delta_1>\delta_2$, where $\delta_{n+1}=v_{n+1}-v_n$.

\emph{A convenient form.}  Define
\[
\Phi(\delta)\equiv\max_{z\ge0}\bigl\{-\omega^{*}G(z)+z\delta\bigr\} ,
\]
which is convex and nondecreasing, with $\Phi'(\delta)=z^{*}(\delta)$.  Writing
$\bar\rho\equiv\rho+z_{-1}^{*}+\kappa$, the value functions \bk{(14.78)}--\bk{(14.79)} become
\[
\bar\rho v_n=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-n}\bigr)+\Phi(\delta_{n+1})+\bigl(z_{-1}^{*}+\kappa\bigr)v_0
\quad (n\ge1),
\]
\[
\bar\rho v_0=\Phi(\delta_{1})+z_0^{*}\bigl(v_{-1}-v_0\bigr)+\bigl(z_{-1}^{*}+\kappa\bigr)v_0 .
\]
Subtracting consecutive equations,
\[
\bar\rho\,\delta_1=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-1}\bigr)+\Phi(\delta_2)-\Phi(\delta_1)
-z_0^{*}\bigl(v_{-1}-v_0\bigr),
\qquad
\bar\rho\,\delta_2=\bigl(\lambda^{-1}-\lambda^{-2}\bigr)+\Phi(\delta_3)-\Phi(\delta_2) .
\]

\emph{The argument.}  Suppose, for contradiction, that $\delta_1\le\delta_2$.  Then
$\Phi(\delta_2)-\Phi(\delta_1)\ge0$, and $v_{-1}\le v_0$ by \bk{Proposition 14.8} gives
$-z_0^{*}(v_{-1}-v_0)\ge0$, so
\[
\bar\rho\,\delta_1\ \ge\ 1-\lambda^{-1} .
\]
On the other hand $\delta_3<\delta_2$ by the part of \bk{Proposition 14.9} already proved, so
$\Phi(\delta_3)\le\Phi(\delta_2)$ and
\[
\bar\rho\,\delta_2\ \le\ \lambda^{-1}-\lambda^{-2}=\lambda^{-1}\bigl(1-\lambda^{-1}\bigr)
\ <\ 1-\lambda^{-1} ,
\]
since $\lambda>1$.  Combining, $\bar\rho\delta_2<\bar\rho\delta_1$, that is $\delta_2<\delta_1$
--- contradicting the hypothesis.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \delta_1>\delta_2\qquad\text{and therefore}\qquad z_0^{*}>z_1^{*} .\ }
\]

\emph{And $z_0^{*}\ge z_{-1}^{*}$.}  Write $d\equiv v_0-v_{-1}\ge0$.  From \bk{(14.80)},
$\rho v_{-1}=\Phi(d)+\kappa d$, and from the $v_0$ equation above,
$\rho v_0=\Phi(\delta_1)+z_0^{*}(v_{-1}-v_0)$.  Subtracting,
\[
\bigl(\rho+z_0^{*}+\kappa\bigr)d=\Phi(\delta_1)-\Phi(d) .
\]
If $d>\delta_1$ the right side would be nonpositive (as $\Phi$ is nondecreasing) while the left
side is strictly positive --- impossible.  So $d\le\delta_1$, and therefore
$z_{-1}^{*}=G'^{-1}(d/\omega^{*})\le G'^{-1}(\delta_1/\omega^{*})=z_0^{*}$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The economics.}  Two forces make neck-and-neck firms the most aggressive innovators.  The
flow profit $1-\lambda^{-n}$ is concave in $n$, so the first step ahead is worth far more than any
later one --- moving from a zero markup to a markup of $\lambda$ is a bigger prize than moving
from $\lambda^{4}$ to $\lambda^{5}$.  And a neck-and-neck firm that fails to innovate risks its
rival innovating instead, which is a loss as well as a foregone gain.  This is the
``escape-competition'' effect, and it is the ingredient missing from the models of Sections
14.1--14.3, where the reward to innovation does not depend on how close the competitor is.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.33}{}
\emph{(a) $\kappa=0$ freezes the economy.}  With $\kappa=0$ a follower can close the gap only by
innovating, and by \bk{Proposition 14.8} there is an $n^{*}$ beyond which the leader does no R\&D
either.  Consider the states $n\ge n^{*}$.  The transition equations \bk{(14.68)} with
$\kappa=0$ and $z_n^{*}=0$ say that such an industry leaves state $n$ only if the follower
innovates, at rate $z_{-1}^{*}$.  So the question is whether followers keep investing.

They do not, under the exercise's conditions.  With $\kappa=0$ and $z_{-1}^{*}=0$, \bk{(14.80)}
gives $\rho v_{-1}=0$, so $v_{-1}=0$; and the follower's optimality condition \bk{(14.82)} then
requires only $\omega^{*}G'(0)\ge v_0$.  When $G'(0)$ is large enough relative to the value of
being neck-and-neck --- which is what the two displayed inequalities of the exercise say, once
$(1-\lambda)$ is read as $1-\lambda^{-1}$, the flow profit of a one-step leader --- this holds and
$z_{-1}^{*}=0$.

Then every state $n\ge n^{*}$ is \emph{absorbing}.  Industries with $n<n^{*}$ keep moving up as
their leaders innovate, and none ever comes back, so the stationary distribution places all its
mass on $\{n\ge n^{*}\}$:
\[
\mu_n^{*}=0\ \text{ for }n<n^{*},
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g^{*}=\log\lambda\Bigl[2\mu_0^{*}z_0^{*}+\sum_{n\ge1}\mu_n^{*}z_n^{*}\Bigr]=0
\]
by \bk{(14.88)}, since $z_n^{*}=0$ wherever $\mu_n^{*}>0$.  The economy ends up with every
industry a permanent monopoly, and growth ceases. $\blacksquare$

\emph{(b) $\kappa>0$ restores growth.}  A positive patent expiry rate makes every state
transition to $n=0$ at rate at least $\kappa$, so no state is absorbing and the stationary
distribution has $\mu_0^{*}>0$.  By \bk{Proposition 14.9}, $z_0^{*}>z_n^{*}$ for every $n\ge1$,
so the industries that $\kappa$ keeps returning to the neck-and-neck state are exactly the ones
that do the most R\&D, and \bk{(14.88)} gives $g^{*}>0$.

\emph{The contrast.}  In the baseline Schumpeterian model of Section 14.1 --- and in the expanding
variety model of Section 13.1.6 --- weakening intellectual property rights unambiguously reduces
growth, because the only thing patents do is deliver rents to innovators, and rents are what
finance R\&D.  Here patents do something else as well: they entrench leads.  A permanent patent
lets leaders accumulate gaps so large that neither they nor their rivals have any reason to
innovate, and the economy grinds to a halt.  Allowing patents to expire keeps industries
competitive, and competitive industries innovate hardest.

There are therefore two effects of $\kappa$ pulling in opposite directions: the standard
\emph{disincentive} effect, which lowers $v_{n+1}-v_n$ and hence every $z_n^{*}$, and a
\emph{composition} effect, which shifts $\mu^{*}$ toward the low-gap states where R\&D is
intense.  Part (a) is the extreme case in which the composition effect is everything.  This is why
the empirical finding that more competitive industries grow faster --- which Sections 14.1--14.3
cannot accommodate --- is consistent with step-by-step innovation.

\rmk{The exercise as printed writes $G'(0)<(1-\lambda)/\rho$ and
$z^{*}\equiv G'^{-1}((1-\lambda)/\rho)$.  Since $\lambda>1$ these are negative, and
$G'^{-1}$ of a negative number is not defined; the intended quantity is $1-\lambda^{-1}$, the flow
profit of a one-step leader from \bk{(14.69)}, which is what makes the displayed conditions
comparisons of a marginal cost of R\&D with a value.}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch14_schumpeterian.pdf}
\caption{Left (Exercises 14.8 and 14.10): equilibrium and Pareto optimal growth in the baseline
model of Section 14.1, against the size of a quality step.  The vertical line is the drastic
threshold $\lambda^{*}$; the optimum lies above the equilibrium at every admissible $\lambda$.
Right (Exercise 14.25): the same comparison in the model with incumbents and entrants, against the
productivity of entry.  More entry lowers the equilibrium growth rate, and the planner's
allocation dominates throughout.}
\label{fig:ch14}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.34}{}
\emph{(a) and (b) The value functions.}  A follower who innovates --- now at the flow rate
$z_{-1}$ from its own R\&D, plus $\kappa$ from patent expiry --- may license the leader's
technology and emerge one step \emph{ahead}, paying $\zeta$ (normalized by $Y$, so that $\zeta$ is
measured in the same units as the $v_n$).  The old leader becomes the follower and receives
$\zeta$.  The normalized value functions of \bk{(14.78)}--\bk{(14.80)} become
\[
\rho v_{-1}=\max_{z_{-1}}\Bigl\{-\omega^{*}G(z_{-1})
+\bigl(z_{-1}+\kappa\bigr)\bigl[v_{1}-\zeta-v_{-1}\bigr]\Bigr\},
\]
\[
\rho v_n=\max_{z_n}\Bigl\{\bigl(1-\lambda^{-n}\bigr)-\omega^{*}G(z_n)
+z_n\bigl[v_{n+1}-v_n\bigr]
+\bigl(z_{-1}^{*}+\kappa\bigr)\bigl[v_{-1}+\zeta-v_n\bigr]\Bigr\},
\qquad n\ge1 ,
\]
with the neck-and-neck value \bk{(14.79)} unchanged.  The R\&D decisions are
\[
z_{-1}^{*}=\max\Bigl\{G'^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{v_1-\zeta-v_{-1}}{\omega^{*}}\Bigr),0\Bigr\},
\qquad
z_n^{*}=\max\Bigl\{G'^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{v_{n+1}-v_n}{\omega^{*}}\Bigr),0\Bigr\} ,
\]
and the steady state is completed by the flow equations \bk{(14.84)}--\bk{(14.86)}, with the
transition out of state $n$ now going to state $1$ rather than state $0$, the labor market
condition \bk{(14.87)}, and the growth rate \bk{(14.88)}.

For licensing to be used, both sides must gain relative to the no-licence outcome, which requires
\[
\underbrace{v_1-v_0}_{\text{follower's gain from leapfrogging rather than levelling}}
\ \ge\ \zeta\ \ge\
\underbrace{v_{-1}\ \text{forgone}-v_{-1}}_{\text{leader's loss, zero here}} ,
\]
so any $\zeta\in(0,\delta_1]$ is mutually acceptable: the follower pays at most the extra value of
emerging ahead, and the leader --- who was going to lose its lead in either case --- accepts
anything positive.

\emph{(c) Why licensing raises growth.}  Two channels, both operating through the composition of
industries.  First, licensing makes catching up \emph{cheaper} for the follower: it obtains the
leader's know-how instead of having to reproduce it, so $z_{-1}^{*}$ rises and gaps close faster.
Second, and more important, the state to which an industry returns is $n=1$ rather than $n=0$ ---
but the flow out of large gaps is faster, so the stationary distribution shifts mass toward small
$n$, where \bk{Proposition 14.9} says R\&D is most intense.  Since \bk{(14.88)} weights each
state's R\&D by its share $\mu_n^{*}$, aggregate R\&D and growth rise.  Against this stands the
usual disincentive: knowing it can be leapfrogged sooner, the leader's $v_{n+1}-v_n$ falls and its
own R\&D declines.  The licence fee $\zeta$ is what limits that damage, since the leader is
compensated rather than simply expropriated.

\emph{The contrast with Exercise 12.9.}  There, in a static Bertrand market, the innovator could
never gain by licensing: handing a rival the same technology destroys exactly the rents licensing
was supposed to monetize, and a take-it-or-leave-it fee cannot recover more than the monopoly
profit the licence destroys.  Two things differ here.  The environment is \emph{dynamic}: the
leader is going to lose its position anyway, at rate $z_{-1}^{*}+\kappa$, so the licence sells
something that has a limited life rather than a perpetual monopoly, and $\zeta$ is a payment for
rents that were partly going to evaporate.  And the incentive to innovate is
\emph{state-dependent}: what matters for aggregate growth is not only how large the prize is but
\emph{where in the state space} industries spend their time, and licensing moves them to where the
prize is most keenly contested.  Neither feature exists in the one-shot problem of Chapter 12.

\rmk{The same logic explains why the model can rationalize the empirical association between
competition and growth.  In Sections 14.1--14.3 any policy that moves rents from innovators to
others reduces growth one for one.  Once the reward to innovation depends on the technology gap,
policies are evaluated by their effect on the \emph{distribution} of gaps as well as on the level
of rents, and the two can pull in opposite directions.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{15}{Directed Technological Change}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty-one exercises of Chapter 15 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises
15.1--15.5, 15.7--15.10, 15.12--15.17, 15.21--15.23, 15.25, 15.26 and 15.30.  The manual solves
15.6, 15.11, 15.18--15.20, 15.24, 15.27--15.29 and 15.31.  Notation follows the book:
$\varepsilon$ is the elasticity of substitution between the two intermediate goods,
$\sigma\equiv1+(\varepsilon-1)\beta$ the derived elasticity of substitution between the factors,
$\gamma\equiv\gamma_H/\gamma_L$, $\eta\equiv\eta_H/\eta_L$, $\delta$ the degree of state
dependence in Section 15.4, $\lambda$ the spillover parameter in Section 15.5, and $S$ the supply
of scientists.  The numerical parts are computed by \texttt{code/ch15.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{15.1}{}
With
$Y=\bigl[\gamma_L(A_LL)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}+\gamma_H(A_HH)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\bigr]^{\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}}$,
differentiate in $L$:
\[
\frac{\partial Y}{\partial L}
=\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\bigl[\cdot\bigr]^{\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}-1}
\gamma_L\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}(A_LL)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}A_L
=\bigl[\cdot\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}}\gamma_LA_L^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}L^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}
=Y^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}\gamma_LA_L^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}L^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}} ,
\]
using $[\cdot]^{1/(\sigma-1)}=Y^{1/\sigma}$.  The same computation in $H$ gives
$\partial Y/\partial H=Y^{1/\sigma}\gamma_HA_H^{(\sigma-1)/\sigma}H^{-1/\sigma}$, so the common
factor $Y^{1/\sigma}$ cancels in the ratio:
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{MP_H}{MP_L}
=\gamma\Bigl(\frac{A_H}{A_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac{H}{L}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}},
\qquad \gamma\equiv\frac{\gamma_H}{\gamma_L} ,\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.1)}. $\blacksquare$

Three consequences follow by inspection, and they organize the whole chapter.

\emph{$\sigma$ is the elasticity of substitution.}  Taking logarithms,
$\log(MP_H/MP_L)=\text{const}-\sigma^{-1}\log(H/L)$, so
$\sigma=-\bigl[\dd\log(MP_H/MP_L)/\dd\log(H/L)\bigr]^{-1}$, as the footnote to \bk{(15.1)}
records.  The constant-technology relative demand curve is downward sloping with slope $-1/\sigma$.

\emph{Factor-augmenting is not factor-biased.}  The exponent on $A_H/A_L$ is
$(\sigma-1)/\sigma$, so
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial(A_H/A_L)}\frac{MP_H}{MP_L}\ \gtrless\ 0
\qquad\text{according as}\qquad
\sigma\ \gtrless\ 1 .
\]
$H$-augmenting technological change is $H$-\emph{biased} only when the factors are gross
substitutes.  When they are gross complements it is $L$-biased, and when $\sigma=1$ it is neutral.

\emph{Why complementarity reverses the sign.}  With $\sigma<1$ the two factors must be used in
near-fixed proportions, so raising the productivity of $H$ creates an excess supply of
$H$-services and an excess demand for $L$.  In the Leontief limit $\sigma\to0$, starting from
$\gamma_LA_LL=\gamma_HA_HH$, any increase in $A_H$ drives the price of $H$ to zero.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.2}{}
\emph{The technology ratio.}  If both \bk{(15.20)} and \bk{(15.21)} hold as equalities, dividing
gives $\eta_LV_L=\eta_HV_H$, which is \bk{(15.26)}.  Substituting \bk{(15.25)},
\[
\frac{\eta_H}{\eta_L}\,\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}=1
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{N_H}{N_L}=\eta^{\sigma}\gamma^{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\sigma-1} ,
\]
which is \bk{(15.27)}.

\emph{The growth rate.}  Free entry in the $L$ sector with $V_L=\beta p_L^{1/\beta}L/r^{*}$ from
\bk{(15.24)} gives $r^{*}=\eta_L\beta p_L^{1/\beta}L$, so we need $p_L$.  Condition \bk{(15.26)}
reads $\eta_L p_L^{1/\beta}L=\eta_Hp_H^{1/\beta}H$, that is
$p_H=p_L\bigl(\eta_LL/\eta_HH\bigr)^{\beta}$.  Substituting into the price normalization
\bk{(15.10)}, and using $\beta(1-\varepsilon)=-(\sigma-1)$,
\[
p_L^{1-\varepsilon}\Bigl[\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}
+\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\frac{\eta_HH}{\eta_LL}\Bigr)^{\sigma-1}\Bigr]=1
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
p_L^{1-\varepsilon}=\frac{(\eta_LL)^{\sigma-1}}
{\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}(\eta_LL)^{\sigma-1}+\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}(\eta_HH)^{\sigma-1}} .
\]
Since $1-\varepsilon=-(\sigma-1)/\beta$, raising to the power $-1/(\sigma-1)$ gives
$p_L^{1/\beta}$, and
\[
r^{*}=\eta_L\beta L\cdot\frac{\bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}(\eta_HH)^{\sigma-1}
+\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}(\eta_LL)^{\sigma-1}\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}}}{\eta_LL}
=\beta\bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}(\eta_HH)^{\sigma-1}
+\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}(\eta_LL)^{\sigma-1}\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}} .
\]
The Euler equation \bk{(15.22)} then gives \bk{(15.29)},
\[
\boxed{\ g^{*}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\beta\bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}(\eta_HH)^{\sigma-1}
+\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}(\eta_LL)^{\sigma-1}\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}}-\rho\Bigr) .\ }
\]
Note that $r^{*}$ is symmetric in the two sectors --- it is a CES aggregate of the two
\emph{effective} factor supplies $\eta_fF$ --- so the same expression would have followed from
free entry in the $H$ sector.

\emph{The two conditions.}  The first inequality in \bk{(15.28)} says $r^{*}>\rho$, hence
$g^{*}>0$.  The second says $(1-\theta)r^{*}<\rho$; since $r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, this is
equivalent to $(1-\theta)g^{*}<\rho$, which makes
$\int_0^\infty e^{-\rho t}C(t)^{1-\theta}\dd t$ converge.  It is also the transversality
condition \bk{(15.23)}: free entry gives $V_f=1/\eta_f$, so total corporate wealth is
$N_L/\eta_L+N_H/\eta_H$, which grows at $g^{*}$, and \bk{(15.23)} requires $g^{*}<r^{*}$ --- the
same inequality.

\emph{Both free-entry conditions bind.}  Suppose one were slack, say $\eta_HV_H<1$, so that
$Z_H=0$ and $N_H$ were constant while $N_L$ grew.  Then $N_H/N_L$ would fall without bound, and
by \bk{(15.25)} $V_H/V_L$ would rise without bound, so $\eta_HV_H$ would eventually exceed
$\eta_LV_L=1$ --- a contradiction.  Hence in a BGP both hold with equality, and \bk{(15.27)} is
the unique relative technology. $\blacksquare$
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.3}{}
By \bk{(15.25)},
\[
\frac{V_H(t)}{V_L(t)}=\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_H(t)}{N_L(t)}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} ,
\]
which is \emph{strictly decreasing} in $N_H/N_L$, whatever $\sigma$: a larger stock of
$H$-augmenting machines lowers the relative value of inventing one more, because the intermediate
good $Y_H$ becomes relatively cheap.

Suppose $N_H(0)/N_L(0)<(N_H/N_L)^{*}$ as given by \bk{(15.27)}.  Then
\[
\eta_HV_H(0)>\eta_LV_L(0) ,
\]
so the two free-entry conditions \bk{(15.20)}--\bk{(15.21)} cannot both hold as equalities: the
one that binds is the $H$ condition, $\eta_HV_H=1$, and $\eta_LV_L<1$.  Hence $Z_H(t)>0$ and
$Z_L(t)=0$: all research is directed at $H$-augmenting machines.  This raises $N_H/N_L$, which
lowers $V_H/V_L$, until the ratio reaches $(N_H/N_L)^{*}$, at which point both conditions hold as
equalities and research is split between the two sectors so as to keep the ratio there.  The
symmetric argument applies when $N_H(0)/N_L(0)>(N_H/N_L)^{*}$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Uniqueness.}  Along the transition only one sector receives research, so the path of
$N_H/N_L$ is pinned down; the level of $N_L+N_H$ and the split of output between consumption and
R\&D follow from the Euler equation and the transversality condition exactly as in Exercise 13.10.

\rmk{The dynamics are unusually simple: the relative technology moves monotonically and the
economy specializes completely in one direction of research until balance is restored.  That is a
consequence of the lab-equipment specification, in which the two research technologies are
perfectly substitutable uses of the final good.  Exercise 15.11 shows that with knowledge
spillovers and enough state dependence the same comparison can instead be \emph{destabilizing},
sending $N_H/N_L$ to zero or infinity.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.4}{}
Substitute \bk{(15.27)} into the constant-technology relative wage \bk{(15.19)}:
\[
\omega^{*}=\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}
=\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl[\eta^{\sigma}\gamma^{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\sigma-1}\Bigr]^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}} .
\]
Collecting the exponents: on $\eta$, $\sigma\cdot\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}=\sigma-1$; on $\gamma$,
$\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}+\frac{\varepsilon(\sigma-1)}{\sigma}=\varepsilon$; and on $H/L$,
\[
\frac{(\sigma-1)^{2}}{\sigma}-\frac{1}{\sigma}=\frac{\sigma^{2}-2\sigma}{\sigma}=\sigma-2 .
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \omega^{*}=\eta^{\sigma-1}\gamma^{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\sigma-2}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.30)}. $\blacksquare$

The exponent $\sigma-2$ is the whole content of \bk{Proposition 15.4}.  It exceeds the
constant-technology exponent $-1/\sigma$ for every $\sigma>0$, since
$\sigma-2+1/\sigma=(\sigma-1)^{2}/\sigma\ge0$: the endogenous-technology relative demand curve is
always \emph{shallower} than the constant-technology one, which is \bk{Proposition 15.3}.  And it
is positive --- an upward-sloping relative demand curve --- exactly when $\sigma>2$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.5}{}
Write $A_f\equiv\eta_fF$ for the effective supply of factor $f$ and
\[
r^{*}=\beta\,R,
\qquad
R\equiv\bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}A_H^{\sigma-1}+\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}A_L^{\sigma-1}
\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}} ,
\]
and recall $\gamma_L+\gamma_H=1$, so raising $\gamma=\gamma_H/\gamma_L$ means raising $\gamma_H$
and lowering $\gamma_L$.  Then
\[
\frac{\partial R^{\sigma-1}}{\partial\gamma_H}
=\varepsilon\Bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon-1}A_H^{\sigma-1}-\gamma_L^{\varepsilon-1}A_L^{\sigma-1}\Bigr] ,
\]
and since $R=\bigl(R^{\sigma-1}\bigr)^{1/(\sigma-1)}$,
\[
\boxed{\ \sgn\frac{\partial g^{*}}{\partial\gamma_H}
=\sgn(\sigma-1)\cdot\sgn\Bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon-1}A_H^{\sigma-1}
-\gamma_L^{\varepsilon-1}A_L^{\sigma-1}\Bigr] .\ }
\]

\emph{A cleaner statement.}  Let
$s_H\equiv\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}A_H^{\sigma-1}/\bigl[\gamma_H^{\varepsilon}A_H^{\sigma-1}
+\gamma_L^{\varepsilon}A_L^{\sigma-1}\bigr]$ be the $H$ sector's share of the aggregate inside
$R$.  Then the bracket is positive if and only if $s_H/\gamma_H>s_L/\gamma_L$, that is if and only
if $s_H>\gamma_H$.  So
\[
\frac{\partial g^{*}}{\partial\gamma}>0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\text{either }\sigma>1\text{ and }s_H>\gamma_H,
\quad\text{or }\sigma<1\text{ and }s_H<\gamma_H .
\]
Recall $\sgn(\sigma-1)=\sgn(\varepsilon-1)$.  With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta_HH=\eta_LL$ the program
gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\varepsilon & \gamma_H & s_H & s_H>\gamma_H & \partial r^{*}/\partial\gamma_H\\
0.5 & 0.2 & 0.333 & \text{yes} & -0.257\\
0.5 & 0.8 & 0.667 & \text{no} & +0.257\\
2.0 & 0.2 & 0.059 & \text{no} & -0.816\\
2.0 & 0.8 & 0.941 & \text{yes} & +0.816
\end{array}
\]

\emph{Why it is ambiguous.}  The growth rate is driven by the interest rate, which is the return
to R\&D, and in a BGP that return is the same in both sectors.  Raising $\gamma_H$ shifts demand
from $Y_L$ to $Y_H$: it raises the profitability of $H$-research and lowers that of $L$-research.
Whether the common return rises depends on which sector was contributing more --- and on whether
the two are substitutes or complements.  With gross substitutes ($\sigma>1$) the aggregate is
dominated by the \emph{larger} sector, so tilting weight toward a sector that already accounts for
more than its weight raises $r^{*}$.  With gross complements the aggregate is dominated by the
\emph{smaller} sector --- the bottleneck --- so the same tilt lowers it.  There is nothing special
about $\gamma$ here: it is the familiar fact that a CES aggregate responds to a reweighting in a
direction that depends on the elasticity.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.7}{}
Under \bk{(15.31)}, one scientist employed on $L$-augmenting research generates new machine
varieties at the flow rate
\[
\frac{\partial\dt N_L(t)}{\partial S_L(t)}=\eta_LN_L(t)^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}N_H(t)^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}} ,
\]
each worth $V_L(t)$ by \bk{(15.8)}.  Her cost is the market wage of scientists, $w_S(t)$.  With
free entry into research, the value of hiring one more scientist cannot be strictly positive, and
must be exactly zero wherever research is actually undertaken:
\[
\boxed{\
\eta_LN_L^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}V_L(t)\le w_S(t),
\quad\text{with equality if }S_L(t)>0 ,\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.33)}, and symmetrically \bk{(15.34)} with the exponents interchanged.

\emph{Intuition.}  Scientists are a \emph{scarce} factor with a single market wage, so the two
research sectors bid against each other for them.  In any equilibrium with research in both
directions their marginal value products must be equal, and equal to $w_S$.  What differs between
the two conditions is only the productivity term, and there the exponents matter: with $\delta=0$
both frontiers carry $(N_LN_H)^{1/2}$, so the stock of knowledge helps the two sectors equally and
drops out of the comparison; with $\delta>0$ a large $N_L$ makes $L$-research more productive than
$H$-research, so past research in a direction makes further research in the same direction
cheaper.  That is the state dependence, and it is what distinguishes this section from the
lab-equipment model, where the frontier \bk{(15.7)} carries no $N$ at all.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.8}{}
Dividing \bk{(15.33)} by \bk{(15.34)}, both holding as equalities, the wage $w_S$ cancels and the
half-powers combine:
\[
\frac{\eta_LN_L^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}V_L}
{\eta_HN_L^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}V_H}=1
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\eta_LN_L^{\delta}V_L=\eta_HN_H^{\delta}V_H ,
\]
and in a BGP $V_f=\pi_f/r^{*}$, giving \bk{(15.35)}:
$\eta_LN_L^{\delta}\pi_L=\eta_HN_H^{\delta}\pi_H$.

Now $\pi_H/\pi_L=V_H/V_L$ is given by \bk{(15.25)}, so
\[
\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigl(\frac{N_L}{N_H}\Bigr)^{\delta}
=\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\delta\sigma}{\sigma}}
=\eta\,\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} ,
\]
using $\frac1\sigma-\delta=\frac{1-\delta\sigma}{\sigma}$.  Raising both sides to the power
$\sigma/(1-\delta\sigma)$,
\[
\boxed{\ \Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*}
=\eta^{\frac{\sigma}{1-\delta\sigma}}\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.36)}, and reduces to \bk{(15.27)} at $\delta=0$. $\blacksquare$

Substituting into \bk{(15.19)} and collecting exponents exactly as in Exercise 15.4 gives
\bk{(15.37)}, with the relative wage exponent $\frac{\sigma-2+\delta}{1-\delta\sigma}$; strong
bias therefore requires only $\sigma>2-\delta$, which is \bk{Proposition 15.8}.  The sign of
$1-\delta\sigma$ is what decides whether an increase in $N_H/N_L$ raises or lowers the relative
incentive to do more $H$-research, and hence whether the dynamics are stable --- the subject of
\bk{Proposition 15.6}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.9}{}
\emph{The scientist allocation.}  A BGP requires both technologies to grow at the same rate.  From
\bk{(15.31)},
\[
\frac{\dt N_L}{N_L}=\eta_L\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}S_L ,
\qquad
\frac{\dt N_H}{N_H}=\eta_H\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1-\delta}{2}}S_H ,
\]
so equality gives
\[
\frac{S_H}{S_L}=\frac{\eta_L}{\eta_H}\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{1-\delta} ,
\]
with $N_H/N_L$ pinned down by \bk{(15.36)}.  Together with $S_L+S_H=S$ this determines
$(S_L^{*},S_H^{*})$ uniquely, and substituting \bk{(15.36)} gives \bk{(15.38)}.

\emph{The growth rate.}  Substituting $S_L^{*}=S\eta_H\bigl/\bigl[\eta_H+\eta_L(N_H/N_L)^{1-\delta}\bigr]$
into $\dt N_L/N_L$ and multiplying numerator and denominator by $(N_H/N_L)^{\delta-1}$,
\[
\boxed{\ g^{*}=\frac{\eta_L\eta_H\bigl(N_H/N_L\bigr)^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}}
{\eta_H\bigl(N_H/N_L\bigr)^{\delta-1}+\eta_L}\,S\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.39)}.  It is unique because $N_H/N_L$ is, and strictly positive.

\emph{Transversality.}  In a BGP the profits $\pi_f$ are constant, so $V_f=\pi_f/r^{*}$ is
constant and total corporate wealth $N_LV_L+N_HV_H$ grows at $g^{*}$.  Condition \bk{(15.23)}
therefore requires $g^{*}<r^{*}=\theta g^{*}+\rho$, that is $(1-\theta)g^{*}<\rho$, which is
exactly the hypothesis of \bk{Proposition 15.5}; the same inequality makes utility finite.
$\blacksquare$

Note the structural difference from Section 15.3: there the growth rate was the interest rate
divided by $\theta$ and depended on the \emph{levels} $\eta_fF$; here it is proportional to the
supply of scientists $S$ and does not involve $\theta$ at all, because the R\&D input is a scarce
factor rather than final output.  What $\theta$ now determines is the interest rate, not the
growth rate.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.10}{}
From Exercise 15.9, $S_H^{*}/S_L^{*}=\eta^{-1}(N_H/N_L)^{1-\delta}$, and substituting
\bk{(15.36)} the elasticity with respect to $\eta$ is
\[
\frac{\dd\log(S_H^{*}/S_L^{*})}{\dd\log\eta}
=-1+\frac{\sigma(1-\delta)}{1-\delta\sigma}
=\frac{-(1-\delta\sigma)+\sigma(1-\delta)}{1-\delta\sigma}
=\boxed{\ \frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma}\ }
\]
Assuming $\sigma<1/\delta$, so that the denominator is positive and the dynamics are stable by
\bk{Proposition 15.6}, this is positive if and only if $\sigma>1$.  The program confirms it:
with $\beta=1/2$, $\delta=0.3$, $H/L=0.5$,
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\varepsilon & \sigma & \eta=0.8 & \eta=1.0 & \eta=1.25\\
0.4\ (\sigma<1) & 0.70 & S_H^{*}=0.567 & 0.546 & 0.525\\
2.0\ (\sigma>1) & 1.50 & S_H^{*}=0.344 & 0.391 & 0.441
\end{array}
\]

\emph{Interpretation.}  Two forces act, and the elasticity above is their sum.  A higher $\eta_H$
makes each $H$-scientist more productive, so \emph{fewer} of them are needed to sustain any given
growth rate of $N_H$: that is the $-1$.  But it also raises the equilibrium stock $N_H/N_L$, by
\bk{(15.36)}, and with state dependence a larger $N_H$ makes further $H$-research more productive
still: that is the $+\sigma(1-\delta)/(1-\delta\sigma)$.

Which dominates turns on $\sigma$ because $\sigma$ governs what happens to the price $p_H$ as
$N_H$ expands.  With gross substitutes the expansion of $Y_H$ depresses $p_H$ only mildly, so the
profitability of $H$-research survives and scientists are drawn in.  With gross complements the
expansion depresses $p_H$ sharply --- the economy needs the two intermediates in near-fixed
proportions, so producing more $Y_H$ is of little use --- and the improved research technology is
more than offset: fewer scientists work on $H$.  The same reversal is behind \bk{(15.1)}'s
distinction between factor-augmenting and factor-biased technological change.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.12}{}
Weak equilibrium bias requires that an increase in $H/L$ move $(A_H/A_L)^{(\sigma-1)/\sigma}$
upward, where the factor-augmenting technologies are $A_f\propto N_f$.  By \bk{(15.1)} this is
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd\log(H/L)}\Bigl[\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}\log\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr]\ \ge\ 0 .
\]
From \bk{(15.36)},
\[
\frac{\dd\log(N_H/N_L)}{\dd\log(H/L)}=\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma} ,
\]
so the quantity to be signed is
\[
\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}\cdot\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma}
=\boxed{\ \frac{(\sigma-1)^{2}}{\sigma\,(1-\delta\sigma)}\ \ge\ 0\ }
\]
whenever $1-\delta\sigma>0$, with equality only at $\sigma=1$. $\blacksquare$

The square is the point.  Whether $\sigma$ is above or below one changes \emph{both} the
direction in which $N_H/N_L$ responds to $H/L$ \emph{and} the direction in which $N_H/N_L$ shifts
the relative marginal product --- and the two reversals cancel.  That is why weak bias is so
robust: it holds in the lab-equipment model \bk{(Proposition 15.3)}, with state dependence
\bk{(Proposition 15.7)}, and without scale effects \bk{(Proposition 15.9)}, for every parameter
value.  Economically, it is the statement that the market size effect always dominates the price
effect: a factor that becomes more abundant is a larger market for the technologies that
complement it, and that is what directs research.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.13}{}
\emph{The planner's problem.}  Pricing machines at marginal cost gives
$x_f^{S}=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}p_f^{1/\beta}F$ as in Exercise 15.6, and consumption is then a
function $C(N_L,N_H)$ of the two technology stocks alone --- the R\&D input is scientists, not
output.  The planner chooses the split $S_L\in[0,S]$, with $S_H=S-S_L$, to maximize \bk{(15.2)}
subject to \bk{(15.31)}.  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H=\frac{C(N_L,N_H)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}
+\mu_L\,\eta_LN_L^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}S_L
+\mu_H\,\eta_HN_L^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}S_H .
\]

\emph{The allocation of scientists.}  $\wh H$ is linear in $S_L$, so an interior optimum requires
the two coefficients to be equal:
\[
\mu_L\,\eta_LN_L^{\delta}=\mu_H\,\eta_HN_H^{\delta} ,
\tag{P}
\]
which has exactly the form of the market condition \bk{(15.35)} --- but with the \emph{shadow}
values $\mu_f$ in place of the private values $V_f$.

\emph{The costates.}  Writing $\nu\equiv N_H/N_L$, $g$ for the common growth rate and
$\Xi\equiv\mu_L\dt N_L+\mu_H\dt N_H$, differentiation gives
\[
\rho\mu_L-\dt\mu_L=C^{-\theta}C_{N_L}
+\frac{1+\delta}{2}\,\frac{\mu_L\dt N_L}{N_L}+\frac{1-\delta}{2}\,\frac{\mu_H\dt N_H}{N_L} ,
\]
and symmetrically for $N_H$.  The two terms after the first are what the market ignores: they are
the value of the \emph{spillovers} that a larger $N_L$ confers on future research in both sectors.

\emph{The comparison.}  In a stationary allocation $\mu_H/\mu_L$ is constant, so
$\dt\mu_L/\mu_L=\dt\mu_H/\mu_H$, and subtracting the two costate equations gives
\[
\frac{C^{-\theta}C_{N_H}}{\mu_H}-\frac{C^{-\theta}C_{N_L}}{\mu_L}
=\frac{1-\delta}{2}\,g\Bigl[\frac{S_H^{S}}{S_L^{S}}-\frac{S_L^{S}}{S_H^{S}}\Bigr] .
\]
The market's counterpart is simply $\pi_H/V_H=\pi_L/V_L=r^{*}$, whose right-hand side is zero.
The planner's condition therefore differs from the market's by the bracketed spillover term, which
vanishes only in the knife-edge cases $\delta=1$ or $S_H^{S}=S_L^{S}$.  Hence the stationary
Pareto optimal ratio $(N_H/N_L)^{S}$ differs from the equilibrium \bk{(15.36)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why this differs from Section 15.3.}  There the innovation possibilities frontier
\bk{(15.7)} is $\dt N_f=\eta_fZ_f$: linear in resources, with no dependence on the existing stocks
of knowledge.  A unit of R\&D spending in either sector creates exactly $\eta_f$ new varieties,
whatever has been discovered before, so the \emph{only} wedge between private and social returns
is the monopoly markup --- and that markup is the same constant $1/(1-\beta)$ in both sectors, so
it cancels in the ratio.  The planner accumulates faster than the market (Exercise 15.6) but in
the same proportions, and the optimal ratio is again \bk{(15.27)}.

With knowledge spillovers there is a second wedge, and it does \emph{not} cancel: a unit of
$L$-research raises the productivity of future research in both sectors, and by different amounts
unless $\delta=0$ makes the two frontiers carry the same $(N_LN_H)^{1/2}$.  Even then --- as the
displayed condition shows --- the terms cancel only if the two sectors employ equal numbers of
scientists.  The direction of technological change is therefore inefficient here in a way it is
not in the lab-equipment model, and the inefficiency is greater the larger is $\delta$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.14}{}
With the limited-spillover frontier \bk{(15.40)}, $\dt N_f=\eta_fN_f^{\lambda}S_f$, the growth
rate of each technology is
\[
\frac{\dt N_f(t)}{N_f(t)}=\eta_fN_f(t)^{\lambda-1}S_f(t) .
\]
In a BGP the left side is constant, and a constant fraction of the population works in research,
so $S_f(t)$ grows at the population growth rate $n$.  Taking logarithms and differentiating,
\[
0=(\lambda-1)\,g_N+n
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ g^{*}=g_N=\frac{n}{1-\lambda}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.41)}, requiring $\lambda<1$. $\blacksquare$

Output per capita is $Y/L\propto N$, so it grows at $n/(1-\lambda)$ too, while aggregate output
grows at $n/(1-\lambda)+n$.  This is exactly the mechanism of Section 13.3: with $\lambda<1$
research becomes harder as the stock of knowledge grows, and only a growing population of
researchers can keep the growth rate from falling.  The growth rate depends on demography and on
$\lambda$ alone --- not on $S$, $\eta_f$, $\beta$ or policy --- so the model is semi-endogenous.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.15}{}
With $\lambda=1$ the frontier \bk{(15.40)} becomes $\dt N_f/N_f=\eta_fS_f(t)$.  In a BGP the
allocation of researchers between the two sectors is constant \emph{as a share}, so
$S_f(t)=s_fS(t)$ with $s_f$ constant, and the research population grows with total population at
the rate $n>0$:
\[
\frac{\dt N_f(t)}{N_f(t)}=\eta_fs_fS(0)e^{nt}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty .
\]
The growth rate of technology --- and hence of output and consumption --- increases without bound,
so it cannot be constant: there is no BGP. $\blacksquare$

In fact the situation is worse than a missing BGP.  Since $\dd\log N/\dd t$ grows exponentially,
$\log N(t)$ grows like $e^{nt}$ and $N(t)$ reaches infinity in finite time, so household utility
is infinite and no equilibrium with a well-defined intertemporal allocation exists.  This is the
same explosion the text warns about in Section 13.3, and it is precisely why $\lambda<1$ is
imposed: with proportional spillovers, population growth compounds on itself.

The case $\lambda=1$ with \emph{constant} population is the model of Section 15.4 with extreme
state dependence ($\delta=1$), which does have a BGP --- the difference being that there the
research force is fixed at $S$ rather than growing.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.16}{}
\emph{(15.42).}  With \bk{(15.40)}, a scientist in sector $f$ produces $\eta_fN_f^{\lambda}$
varieties per unit time, so the free-entry conditions are
$\eta_fN_f(t)^{\lambda}V_f(t)\le w_S(t)$ with equality when $S_f>0$.  Dividing the two,
\[
\eta_LN_L^{\lambda}V_L=\eta_HN_H^{\lambda}V_H .
\]
In a BGP, profits grow at the rate of population growth $n$ (the market for each machine is the
factor supply), so $V_f=\pi_f/(r^{*}-n)$ by the argument of Exercise 13.18; the common
denominator cancels and
\[
\boxed{\ \eta_LN_L^{\lambda}\pi_L=\eta_HN_H^{\lambda}\pi_H\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.42)}, the analogue of \bk{(15.35)} with $\lambda$ in place of $\delta$.

\emph{(15.43).}  Substituting $\pi_H/\pi_L$ from \bk{(15.25)} and solving, exactly as in
Exercise 15.8 with $\delta$ replaced by $\lambda$,
\[
\boxed{\ \Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*}
=\eta^{\frac{\sigma}{1-\lambda\sigma}}\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\lambda\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac HL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\lambda\sigma}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.43)}; and substituting into \bk{(15.19)} gives \bk{(15.44)}, with relative-wage
exponent $(\sigma-2+\lambda)/(1-\lambda\sigma)$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{What the section establishes.}  The exponent structure is identical to Section 15.4 with
$\lambda$ playing the role of $\delta$, even though the growth rate is now $n/(1-\lambda)$ and
carries no scale effect at all.  The market size effect that governs the \emph{direction} of
technological change and the scale effect that governs its \emph{rate} are therefore entirely
separable: one can remove the counterfactual scale effect without touching the weak and strong
bias results.  That is the point of the section, and it matters because the scale effect is the
most criticized feature of endogenous growth models while the bias results are the ones the
chapter is trying to defend.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.17}{}
\emph{(a) Equilibrium.}  Labor market clearing is now
\[
H^{E}(t)+H^{R}_{L}(t)+H^{R}_{H}(t)\le H,
\qquad
L^{E}(t)+L^{R}_{L}(t)+L^{R}_{H}(t)\le L ,
\]
and both research sectors combine the two factors with the same constant-returns technology
$\mathcal R(H^{R},L^{R})$.  Let
\[
c\bigl(w_L(t),w_H(t)\bigr)
\]
be the unit cost function of that technology --- the cost of one unit of ``research input''.
Since $\mathcal R$ is the same in both sectors, $c$ is too.  The free-entry conditions replace
$w_S$ in \bk{(15.33)}--\bk{(15.34)} by $c$:
\[
\eta_LN_L^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}V_L(t)\le c\bigl(w_L,w_H\bigr),
\qquad
\eta_HN_L^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}N_H^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}V_H(t)\le c\bigl(w_L,w_H\bigr) ,
\]
each with equality when the corresponding research activity is positive.  An equilibrium is a path
of consumption, machine prices and quantities, technologies, research inputs and factor prices
satisfying these together with \bk{(15.10)}, \bk{(15.19)}, \bk{(15.22)} and \bk{(15.23)}.

\emph{(b) The BGP.}  Dividing the two free-entry conditions, the common cost $c$ cancels exactly
as $w_S$ did, leaving
\[
\eta_LN_L^{\delta}V_L=\eta_HN_H^{\delta}V_H ,
\]
which is \bk{(15.35)}.  Hence \emph{the relative technology is again \bk{(15.36)}}, with $H/L$
replaced by the ratio of the factors actually employed in final good production,
\[
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*}
=\eta^{\frac{\sigma}{1-\delta\sigma}}\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{H^{E}}{L^{E}}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma}} ,
\]
and $H^{E}/L^{E}$ is itself determined by the research sector's factor demands at the equilibrium
$w_H/w_L$.  The growth rate is determined as in Exercise 15.9, with the quantity of research input
replacing $S$.

\emph{(c) Bias.}  Since the relative technology condition is unchanged in form, the derivations of
Exercises 15.4 and 15.12 go through verbatim with $H^{E}/L^{E}$ in place of $H/L$: weak
equilibrium bias holds always, with the elasticity $(\sigma-1)^{2}/[\sigma(1-\delta\sigma)]\ge0$,
and strong bias holds when $\sigma>2-\delta$.  The analogues of \bk{Propositions 15.3} and
\bk{15.4} therefore hold.

\emph{(d) Transitional dynamics.}  The comparison driving Exercise 15.3 is unchanged: $V_H/V_L$
is decreasing in $N_H/N_L$, so when $\sigma<1/\delta$ research is directed entirely at whichever
technology is scarce until the ratio \bk{(15.36)} is restored, and both conditions then hold with
equality.  When $\sigma>1/\delta$ the comparison reverses and the dynamics are divergent, as in
\bk{Proposition 15.6}.

\emph{(e) The optimum.}  The planner's allocation is characterized as in Exercise 15.13, with
the research input in place of scientists.  Its first-order condition for the direction of
research again takes the form $\mu_L\eta_LN_L^{\delta}=\mu_H\eta_HN_H^{\delta}$, and the costate
equations again carry spillover terms absent from the market's.  The stationary ratio therefore
coincides with the lab-equipment expression \bk{(15.27)} only when those terms cancel --- which
happens when $\delta=0$ \emph{and} the two sectors employ equal research inputs, and in the
knife-edge case $\delta=1$.  The substantive point stands either way: the \emph{direction} of
technological change is governed by \bk{(15.36)}, and the fact that R\&D uses produced factors
rather than a separate stock of scientists changes none of the bias results.

\rmk{The exercise is a robustness check with a clear moral.  What drives the direction of
technological change is the comparison of the \emph{values} $V_L$ and $V_H$ against research costs
that are \emph{common to the two sectors}.  Anything that leaves that symmetry intact --- final
output as in Section 15.3, scientists as in Section 15.4, or ordinary labor as here --- delivers
the same relative technology condition and the same bias results.  What would break them is a
research technology whose factor intensity differed systematically between the two directions,
which is a question the chapter does not take up.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.21}{}
\emph{(a)}  Let $H(t)=K(t)$ with $\dt K/K=s_K>0$ by \bk{(15.45)}, and let $\delta<1$.  In any BGP
with research in both directions, \bk{(15.36)} holds:
\[
\frac{N_K(t)}{N_L(t)}
=\eta^{\frac{\sigma}{1-\delta\sigma}}\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{K(t)}{L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{1-\delta\sigma}} .
\]
Since $K(t)$ grows at $s_K$, the relative technology must grow at
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log\frac{N_K}{N_L}=\frac{(\sigma-1)s_K}{1-\delta\sigma}\ \neq\ 0
\qquad\text{unless }\sigma=1 .
\]
So the two technologies grow at different rates.  But balanced growth with $K$ growing requires
Harrod neutrality --- all technological change asymptotically labor-augmenting, that is
$\dt N_K=0$.  That is incompatible with an interior BGP: with $\delta<1$ the free-entry conditions
hold as equalities in both sectors, so $S_K>0$ and $N_K$ strictly grows.  Hence no BGP exists.
$\blacksquare$

\emph{(b)}  Suppose consumption grew at a constant rate.  By \bk{(15.22)} the interest rate would
then be constant.  But the interest rate is the rental rate of capital, and by \bk{(15.19)} with
$H=K$,
\[
r(t)=w(t)\,\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_K(t)}{N_L(t)}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac{K(t)}{L}\Bigr)^{-\frac1\sigma} ,
\]
so $r$ can be constant only if $N_K$ and $K$ move together in a particular way.  Repeating the
argument of part (a), an equilibrium with research in both directions forces $N_K(t)$ to grow
without bound, and then the term in $N_K$ drives $r(t)$ monotonically --- the capital-augmenting
